# Chapter 1 — The Pain Came First

The pain came first.

Wrong pain. The kind that didn't belong to injury, that had no source and no direction—like being poured into something that was already full, everything sloshing into places it shouldn't fit.

Stone slammed into Rachel's ribs. The breath ripped out of her. Cold grit scraped her cheek and she didn't understand, couldn't understand, because a second ago she'd been—

Where had she been?

Lines carved into the floor burned white-hot against her skin. Too bright. Too hot. The world snapped sideways and stayed that way, refusing to settle, and Rachel's thoughts scattered like something had knocked them loose.

Someone screamed nearby, high and cracking. Another voice cut through it, barking orders in sounds that felt sharp in the mouth even though she couldn't—shouldn't—none of this was—

"No - don't anchor it yet -"

Something yanked at her spine. Behind her eyes. Hooked deep and pulling forward like it wanted to drag her inside out.

*This isn't real*, she thought, or tried to think, but the thought kept sliding sideways.

Rachel tried to breathe and tasted blood. Thick and coppery. She gagged and swallowed it anyway because spitting felt like giving up and she didn't even know what she'd be giving up on.

Hands grabbed her arms. Rough. Gloved. Fingers digging in too hard, like they thought she might dissolve if they let go, and Rachel wanted to scream *get off me, who are you, where am I* but her mouth wouldn't make the shapes.

A face leaned into view—young, pale, spattered with dark spots that looked wet.

"You are not supposed to be this early," the man said.

The words landed wrong. She understood them even though she shouldn't have, and that was its own kind of terrifying.

His voice shook. So did his hands.

The circle flared.

She knew the cadence of it somehow. Hated that she did. Hated that her body recognized something her mind had never seen.

Light crawled along the carved lines, ugly-colored, wrong-colored, the kind that made her teeth ache and her stomach turn. The pull intensified, dragging at her chest like it wanted everything inside her out at once, heart and lungs and all of it.

Someone shouted, "One anchor is dead - cut it, cut it -"

The dead man lay half inside the circle.

His legs were gone. Just—gone. Ended cleanly, sliced away by something impossibly thin. The rest of him twitched when the light pulsed, small useless movements, and Rachel's brain finally caught up to what her eyes were seeing and *rejected it entirely*.

This wasn't possible. People didn't just—this wasn't—

She tried to pull away.

The hands holding her tightened.

She screamed.

Not words. Not even a sound she recognized. Just everything that had nowhere else to go tearing its way out through her throat—the dead man, the circle, the wrongness of understanding words she'd never learned, all of it compressed into one raw noise that scraped her vocal cords bloody.

Something answered.

The air bent.

For a fraction of a second the room flipped, floor above and ceiling below, and then gravity slammed back into place wrong. Bones cracked. Someone hit the wall hard enough to leave a red smear that slid slowly downward. A woman lifted off her feet, spun once, and folded backward around nothing, her scream cutting off in the middle.

The circle shattered.

Stone blew upward in chunks. The pull snapped back on itself like a broken tendon and Rachel went with it, thrown clear, skidding across the floor until her shoulder hit a column hard enough to turn the edges of the room black.

She lay there gasping, ears ringing, lungs burning, light still crawling over her skin. It did not fade. It sank in, slow and heavy.

The quiet did not last.

Alarms started howling. Deep ones. Layered ones. The kind that crawled into your teeth and stayed there. Doors slammed open somewhere nearby. Boots pounded closer, too many of them, coming fast.

"Containment breach!" someone yelled. "It is unstable - do not let it touch the -"

Rachel rolled onto her side and tried to push herself up.

Her arms shook. Her chest hurt in a way that felt structural, like something inside had shifted and not found its way back.

Across the chamber, the young summoner stared at her.

Horror hollowed his face out.

"You are not supposed to survive the break," he said.

The words landed heavy. *Break*. Like she was something that had been torn open. Like she was the disaster here, not the dead man with no legs, not the woman folded backward around nothing.

*I was just—I was somewhere else. I was home. I was—*

She couldn't finish the thought. Couldn't remember what home looked like, which terrified her more than anything else.

Crossbow bolts slammed into the stone around her.

One punched through her leg like it barely noticed the bone. Pain detonated, bright and blinding, and Rachel screamed again as she staggered sideways, nearly going down.

She didn't think. Couldn't think. Her brain had given up on processing and gone straight to *make it stop, make it stop, make it stop*—

The light surged outward.

The nearest guard vanished.

Just—gone. One second there, the next erased so completely the air didn't even rush to fill the space. The wall behind him folded inward with a shriek, stone compressing into itself, cracking and collapsing.

Rachel stared at her hands. *Did I do that?*

Dust and fragments blasted through the chamber. People went down. Someone cried. Someone prayed.

"Kill it now!" a voice shouted.

Another voice cut in, older and steadier.

"No. It is breached but coherent. If it gets out -"

The iron doors at the far end of the chamber buckled.

Once.

Twice.

Cracks raced through the metal like veins as something on the other side slammed into them again and again, hard enough to bow the frame.

Drawn by the noise. By the rupture.

The young summoner backed away, shaking so badly he nearly dropped the knife in his hand.

"We have to collapse the hall," he said. "We have to bury it -"

Rachel dragged herself upright.

Blood slicked the floor under her hands. Her leg screamed every time she moved it. The light under her skin shifted, not flaring, not fading, just present, heavy.

She looked at the doors.

Then at the bodies.

Then down the corridor beyond the chamber, already filling with smoke and falling embers as torches shattered and burned.

She ran.

Bolts chased her, clattering and sparking off stone. One grazed her neck, hot and close. Another shattered a torch, plunging the hall into smoke.

Behind her, the doors finally gave way.

Something screamed, long and hungry.

She didn't look back. Couldn't afford to. Her legs were moving without her permission, which was probably the only reason she was still moving at all.

The corridor bent, bent again, then stopped bending and just went on—straight and narrow and wrong, like it had decided she wasn't meant to reach an end. Like the architecture itself was in on whatever had happened to her.

Her leg screamed every time her foot hit the stone. The bolt was still in there. She could feel it shift when she moved, a wet dragging sensation that made her stomach roll. Blood ran into her boot, warm at first, then slick, her footing growing less reliable with every step.

The air tasted sharp and metallic, like a storm waiting overhead without ever breaking.

Behind her, something struck the walls hard enough to shake dust loose from the ceiling. Close enough to matter. Too close.

Rachel pushed harder, breath tearing in and out of her chest, the corridor stretching ahead of her in a way that made distance feel meaningless. Her bad leg buckled and she nearly went down, her hand slapping the wall to keep her upright.

The stone was warm.

Too warm.

She jerked her hand back.

Light surged under her skin.

The wall puckered inward where she had touched it, stone dimpling like clay pressed by a thumb. A crack raced outward with a dry snapping sound, racing away from her hand and disappearing around the curve of the corridor.

Rachel stared for half a second too long.

She forced herself to move.

The corridor forked.

No markings she understood. No signs. Just left or right, both narrow, both dark, both wrong in different ways.
Spirals scored into the stone—ward marks, not directions. One of them looked familiar and she had no idea why. She'd never seen anything like this in her life, except apparently her body disagreed.

Boots thundered behind her. Voices carried, overlapping and sharp.

"-don't let it corner-"
"-bleeding-"
"-keep it moving-"

*It*.

They were talking about her like she was a thing. Like she was the monster here.

Rachel went right, half because she had to choose and half because fuck them.

The floor dipped almost immediately. Water slicked the stone, old and stagnant, and her bad foot slid out from under her. She went down hard, the breath ripped from her lungs as pain tore up her leg so fast her vision sparked.

She screamed.

Her hands shook as she rolled onto her side, fingers closing around the bolt. The shaft was slick with blood. Her blood. She hesitated for a fraction of a second that felt much longer than it should have.

Then she pulled.

White exploded behind her eyes.

She screamed again, louder, jaw clenched so tight it popped as she yanked harder. The bolt came free with a wet sound that made bile rise in her throat, and blood poured out fast, too fast, soaking the stone beneath her.

"No," she gasped, hands pressing uselessly against the wound. "No, stop."

It did not stop.

The light surged.

Not outward. Down.

The stone beneath her rippled. The water froze, not into clear ice but into something cloudy and brittle that cracked under its own weight. The floor bulged upward, lifting Rachel with it, forcing her onto her feet as the corridor warped and groaned.

The bleeding slowed.

Slower. Something inside her had clenched without asking, and Rachel recognized the sensation—that same wrong mercy from before—and her stomach dropped.

She stood shaking, hands slick with her own blood, staring at her leg.

The wound looked *wrong*.

The flesh had pulled together unevenly, tight and pale. Thin dark lines spread beneath the skin before fading as she watched.

*What am I?*

The question surfaced before she could stop it, and she shoved it back down because she couldn't afford to think about it, couldn't afford to acknowledge that her body was doing things bodies weren't supposed to do.

Behind her, something howled.

Closer now.

Rachel limped forward, the corridor spitting her into a wider hall lined with doors set deep into the walls. Some stood closed. Some were dented. One hung half off its hinges, metal clawed and twisted outward.
Each door carried a thin white knot of paint at shoulder height, a ward sign, not a number.

She slowed despite herself.

A shape moved in one of the open doorways.

Rachel screamed and swung without thinking, without wanting to, her arm moving and the light *answering*—

The shape slammed into the opposite wall and slid down, boneless.

*Oh god. Oh god, I killed someone. Did I kill someone?*

She couldn't stop to check. Couldn't afford to know.

Boots thundered closer. Someone blew a horn, low and vibrating, the sound settling into her bones and staying there. Something answered it from deeper in the structure, a long call that raised the hair along her arms.

The building felt awake.

She burst through another doorway and into open air.

Cold hit her hard, stealing her breath. The sky overhead was dark, clouded, smeared with smoke and drifting embers. Towers rose around her, packed close, windows glowing unevenly. Bells rang everywhere, clanging and overlapping until the city became one huge alarm.

People were running. Away from her. Every single one of them, like she was the disaster, the thing that had gone wrong.

Someone saw her stumble into the street—blood-soaked, faintly glowing beneath the skin—and screamed. The sound spread, jumping from throat to throat.

"Outsider!"
"Break-runner!"
"Get back-"

Shutters slammed. A child was dragged behind a cart. Something heavy shattered at Rachel's feet, glass and ceramic exploding outward.

*I'm not—I didn't do anything—*

But she had. She'd killed at least one person. Maybe more. And she didn't know how to stop whatever was inside her from doing it again.

She staggered forward, dizzy now, the world tilting and blurring at the edges. The light under her skin pulsed with her heartbeat, each thud a little stronger, a little harder to contain.

She felt the pull then.

Not behind her.

Outward.

Thread-thin but insistent, tugging at her chest, pointing her somewhere she did not want to go.

Rachel staggered the other way.

The pull sharpened.

She gasped, clutching at herself as if she could hold it in place. The street stones cracked beneath her boots, fractures racing outward as the light flared again, uncontrolled.

High above, something answered.

A pale arc of light traced across the sky, hanging for a breath too long before snapping downward, pointing straight at her.

Rachel swore and ran.

Behind her, the horns changed.

Sharper. Faster.

She had made it out.

The city had not let her go.

# Chapter 2 — Until the Street Narrowed

Rachel ran until the street narrowed and the buildings leaned in close, until the bells thinned behind her and the horns stretched into something distant and ragged.

The pull didn't fade.

If anything, it tightened—a steady pressure behind her breastbone, patient and exact, drawing her sideways every time she tried to ignore it. Like something had hooked into her chest and was gently, insistently, steering.

"Stop it," she muttered, breath scraping in and out of her chest. "Whatever you are—*stop*."

She tried to cut left, away from the pull.

Pain bloomed behind her eyes. Heavy. Dull. Squeezing until her vision tunneled and her knees wobbled. She staggered, caught herself on a wall rough with old mortar, and swore.

*What the fuck is inside me?*

She moved back the other way.

The pressure eased. Rewarding her, like she was a dog learning a trick.

Rachel wanted to scream. She kept running instead.

The streets changed as she went, stone underfoot growing uneven and old, patched and repatched until it looked like scars layered over scars. The buildings thinned, windows narrowing, doors reinforced with iron bands and deep-cut marks that had nothing decorative about them.

No one ran here.

They watched.

Faces hovered behind shutters. Eyes tracked her from alleys that smelled of damp and rot. The light under Rachel's skin pulsed with her heartbeat, brightening with each step, and with it came a faint shift in the air, like breathing out of time with her own.

She reached a narrow square with a dry fountain at its center and slowed despite herself. Something dark stained the basin—old and flaked. She looked away before her brain could process what it might have been.

The pull sharpened.

*I don't want to go that way.*

It didn't care what she wanted.

Rachel lurched forward, dragged toward a stairwell cut into the side of a building that looked less built than stacked, stones piled until they stayed.

The door at the top stood open.

She stopped. Everything in her screamed *trap*, screamed *wrong*, screamed *don't you dare*.

The pressure didn't punish her for hesitating, but it didn't ease either. Just held there, steady, insistent—making the space behind her feel suddenly too exposed.

Horns sounded again, closer now, faster, the rhythm changed.

Rachel looked back just long enough to see pale light flicker at the far end of the street, shapes moving together with too much coordination to be accidental.

She turned and went down the stairs.

The air changed immediately.

Cooler. Damp. Heavy with mold and a faint sweetness underneath that made her stomach roll. The stairs spiraled down farther than they should have, stone slick under her boots, walls pressing close enough that her shoulders brushed both sides when she lost her balance.

Her leg screamed.

She paused halfway down, hand braced against the wall, breath tearing in and out of her chest.

The pressure softened.

Rachel swallowed and kept going.

At the bottom, the stairwell opened into a low chamber lit by guttering lamps. Iron cages lined the walls, their bars thick and scarred, some empty, some holding shapes that did not stay still. Their skin held a bruised blue cast in the lamplight, too thin, veins showing like ink.

Something shifted.

Rachel froze.

Chains rattled softly. A tall shape moved closer to the bars—joints bending wrong, eyes opening pale in the dark, pupils flat and strange. It smiled, mouth stretching wider than any mouth should, teeth set in a second row.

"Oh," it said, voice low and pleased. "You can see us."

*I'm dreaming.* The thought was desperate, ridiculous. *I hit my head and I'm dreaming and when I wake up I'll be home and none of this will have happened—*

She took a step back.

The pressure yanked.

Hard.

Pain speared through her chest and she stumbled forward despite herself, the light under her skin flaring bright enough to throw warped shadows across the chamber.

The thing laughed.

Another voice joined it. Then another.

From cages she had not noticed, from corners that had been empty seconds before, shapes leaned forward, eyes gleaming, teeth catching the light, their faces lagging half a beat behind their skulls.

"Fresh," someone murmured.
"Uncut," said another.
"Not sealed," breathed a third.

Rachel's leg buckled. She caught herself on a post slick with old grime, heart slamming against her ribs.

"What are you?" she demanded, and hated how her voice cracked. "What is this place? What's *happening* to me?"

They didn't answer. Just kept watching with those too-bright eyes.

The pull shifted her attention to the far end of the chamber, where something large sat half-lost in shadow. A circle carved deep into the stone, older than the one she had broken, its edges worn smooth. Chains anchored it at eight points, disappearing into the floor.

At its center sat a figure.

Not bound. Not caged.

It sat with its hands folded loosely in its lap, bare feet on stone, toes too long, nails clear as glass, long dark hair hanging in tangled ropes down its back.

When it lifted its head, the light under Rachel's skin recoiled.

"You followed," the figure said.

"I didn't—" Rachel's voice was ragged, barely working. "I don't know what's happening. I don't know where I am. I didn't *choose* any of this."

The figure tilted its head slightly, as if her panic was mildly interesting. "You moved."

The chains around the circle trembled.

Above them, horns sounded again, louder now, echoing down through the stone.

The figure stood, movement smooth and unhurried, and stepped closer to the edge of the circle.

"They will burn this place," it said. "They always do."

The pressure tightened again, sharp enough to pull a sound from Rachel's throat.

The figure stopped just short of the boundary, eyes fixed on her.

"You can go back up those stairs," it said. "Or you can stay where the fire reaches last."

The cages rattled.

The things inside pressed forward.

Rachel stood there shaking, blood soaking her boot, light pulsing under her skin, the sound of horns above and breathing below.

She did not step forward.

She did not run.

The pressure waited.

Then it tightened until her chest felt like it was folding inward and her legs moved without her consent.

Rachel stepped forward.

Her boot crossed the line.

Nothing happened.

For half a second the circle stayed dark and dead, the carved stone cold under her sole, and Rachel froze there with her breath locked in her throat, waiting for the snap, the surge, the pain that was supposed to come next.

Then the light slammed upward.

It did not flare so much as surge, thick and heavy, climbing the carved lines like something waking up angry, and the chains screamed as they pulled tight, metal grinding against stone hard enough to spit sparks while the air bent and folded in on itself.

Rachel screamed too.

The sound tore out of her before she could stop it, raw and breaking, as pain ripped through her chest and spine and hooked hard behind her eyes. She dropped to her knees inside the circle, hands slapping uselessly against the stone while the light crawled up her arms and over her throat and into her face, hot and wrong.

She tried to crawl back out.

The pressure crushed down and flattened her, driving the breath out of her lungs and pinning her there with her cheek against the glowing stone.

Something latched on.

Not her body.

Her.

The latch felt deliberate, like a hand closing around her ribs from the inside.

Rachel gasped and choked as heat and cold tore through her head, impressions flashing too fast and too close together to separate, white-hot fragments piling up without order or edge.

The figure at the center of the circle watched without moving.

"Easy," it said. "You will tear if you fight it."

Rachel clawed at the stone, fingers skidding over the glowing lines, skin burning where she touched them.

"I didn't agree to this," she said, the words thick and wrong in her mouth, dragged out of her like something caught on a hook. "I didn't agree to *any* of this—not being here, not whatever this thing inside me is, not—"

The figure smiled.

"No," it said. "You crossed."

"I was *forced*. Something pushed me—"

"You crossed."

The words were patient. Final. Like it didn't matter what she wanted. Like what she wanted had never mattered.

The chains shuddered again, a deep vibration running through the floor.

The circle tightened.

Rachel felt the shape of it then, not flowing through her so much as pressing and pulling, compressing where she resisted and dragging where she yielded, scraping against something inside her that did not want to be touched at all.

Her vision tunneled.

Something screamed.

Something else answered.

The answer did not feel like hers.

The light under her skin changed, not dimming, not flaring, but sinking deeper, growing heavier and slower, pulsing in time with her heartbeat and tugging at her thoughts whenever she tried to move.

Rachel collapsed forward and retched, bile burning her throat as dark flecks spattered the stone inside the circle and hissed faintly where they landed.

The cages erupted.

Chains rattled violently as the things inside pressed forward, claws and hands and too-many-jointed limbs shoving through the bars as far as they could reach.

"She's binding."
"Not sealed - look at her -"
"That one's going to stick."

Rachel forced herself onto her hands and knees, vision swimming, breath coming in ragged pulls that scraped her chest raw.

"What did you do," she said, the words barely holding together.

The figure stepped closer, stopping just short of touching her, close enough that she could feel the air shift.

"What your kind always does," it said. "You stayed."

"I tried to leave."

The figure tilted its head.

"You crossed."

The chains slackened.

Not enough to release.

Enough to wait.

Above them, stone cracked.

Dust sifted down from the ceiling in thin sheets, stinging Rachel's eyes. A heavy impact shook the chamber, then another, followed by shouting that echoed down the stairwell, sharp and coordinated, too practiced to be panic.

"They're here," something in a cage said softly. "Oh. They're really here."

The figure straightened and listened, head cocked slightly.

"They will burn this chamber," it said.

Rachel's heart hammered so hard it hurt.

"Then let me out."

The figure looked down at her.

"No."

The word landed flat and final.

"But," it continued, "I can keep you alive while they try."

The circle flared again, tighter this time, squeezing until Rachel cried out and curled in on herself, the light searing behind her eyes and down her spine.

Stone cracked overhead.

Fragments fell.

Rachel screamed as something inside her shifted and locked, the pressure settling into a new shape that did not move when she did.

Above them, the ceiling began to come apart.

# Chapter 3 — The Ceiling Came Down

The ceiling came down in pieces first—stone cracking, splitting overhead, dust pouring down in choking sheets while heat rolled through the chamber and stole what little breath Rachel had left.

Something screamed above them. Inhuman. Wrong.

Then it cut off, and the silence was worse.

Fire followed—careful, measured, pouring down the stairwell and through cracks in the stone like something being applied on purpose. This wasn't wildfire. Someone was burning this place deliberately.

Rachel curled tighter inside the circle, arms locked over her head, lungs burning as the air thickened and grew hot enough to hurt. The light under her skin pulsed sluggishly now, heavy as syrup, dragging at her thoughts when she tried to move.

The cages screamed.

Metal shrieked as iron bars glowed red, then white, the sound sharp enough to set her teeth on edge. The things inside howled and clawed and begged in voices that twisted words into sounds Rachel could barely recognize.

"Open it!"
"Let us out - let us out -"
"We helped you -"

They burned anyway.

Rachel smelled it, sweet and rancid and thick, coating the back of her throat until she gagged and retched, black flecks spattering the stone inside the circle.

She tried to crawl.

The circle did not let her.

Chains rattled violently as the heat intensified, anchors screaming in protest as the stone around them cracked and shifted. One snapped loose with a sound like a gunshot, whipping free and carving a deep groove through the floor before vanishing into the smoke.

The figure stood unmoving at the center of it all.

Fire washed over its back and broke around it like water around a rock, leaving the stone beneath its feet blackened but intact.

"Breathe," it said calmly. "If you pass out, you die."

"I thought -" Rachel choked, lungs spasming. "You said -"

"I said I would teach you how not to die," it replied. "This is part of it."

The floor lurched.

Stone gave way beneath one side of the chamber, collapsing inward as the ceiling finally failed. A section of wall tore loose, exposing a yawning shaft beyond, black and deep, swallowing heat and sound as fast as they came.

The pull inside Rachel twisted.

Not toward the figure.

Toward the hole.

She screamed as the circle loosened, not released, just weakened enough to slip, and the light surged outward in a ragged, desperate pulse that made the air scream.

Chains tore free.

The circle ruptured.

Rachel was thrown forward as the stone beneath her feet vanished. She tumbled end over end into open air, hit hard, and slid down a steep incline of broken masonry, skin scraping raw, lungs screaming for breath that would not come.

She did not stop until something caught her by the ankle.

Rachel shrieked and kicked blindly, pain flaring bright and hot as her foot twisted.

The grip loosened.

She scrambled away on hands and knees, coughing, eyes streaming, the heat finally fading behind her as darkness closed in.

The tunnel leveled out and spat her onto cold stone slick with moisture.

Rachel lay there gasping, chest heaving, every nerve screaming, the taste of smoke and ash thick in her mouth.

Behind her, the chamber collapsed fully.

The sound rolled through the earth like thunder, long and deep and final.

Rachel laughed—a broken, hysterical sound that tore out of her before she could stop it.

*I'm alive. How am I alive? I should be dead. I should be burned. I should be—*

Then she noticed her leg.

Not pain. Something deeper. Something that felt like her body rearranging itself without asking permission.

She pushed herself upright and looked down.

The wound where the bolt had been—gone. Not healed over, not scarred, just *gone*, replaced by smooth darkened flesh and a faint circular mark that pulsed in time with her heartbeat.

Rachel touched it before she could stop herself.

The light under her skin flared, and with it came attention. Cold. Patient. Something that was definitely not her looking out through her own eyes.

She jerked her hand back, heart pounding.

*What did they put in me? What am I becoming?*

The question had no answer. The thing inside her just settled back, waiting.

Footsteps echoed faintly somewhere above, voices shouting, distant but spreading, the sound of search rather than panic.

Rachel forced herself to stand.

Smoke still poured from the tunnel mouth behind her, heat bleeding through the stone in slow waves. She limped into the darkness, each step unsteady, the pull inside her quiet but not gone.

Behind her, the ones in the cages burned.

Whatever she was now kept moving.

She climbed until her lungs burned and every breath tasted like ash, the tunnel refusing to behave like a tunnel ought to, splitting and narrowing and slanting just wrong, as if it was less interested in letting her out than in seeing how long she would keep trying.

Smoke followed her in thin, lazy ropes, never close enough to choke her, never far enough to forget.

Her leg did not throb the way it should have, and that was the problem. It should have been screaming at her, open and raw and furious, demanding attention, but instead it sat in her awareness like a hot coin pressed to the skin, warm and heavy and wrong in a way that made her stomach uneasy.

She kept moving anyway, because stopping felt too much like lying down, and lying down felt like dying.

The pull sat in her chest, quieter now, different, not pointing so much as resting there, a hand laid flat against her sternum with the fingers curled just tight enough to remind her it had not gone anywhere.

She hit a narrow rise and nearly went down when her bad foot slipped. Her hand shot out on instinct and slapped against the wall.

The nausea hit so fast it stole her breath.

Her vision tunneled. Black crept in from the edges, thick and heavy, and for a horrible second she thought this was it, that she was going to pass out right there, folded up in the dark with smoke creeping closer and no one even needing to finish the job.

Rachel jerked her hand away and sagged down onto her knees, gagging.

"Okay," she whispered, shaking. "Okay."

The nausea eased. Not because she had asked. Because whatever lived under her skin had decided it had made its point.

She pushed on slower, testing each step, the ground suddenly feeling like something that might notice if she chose wrong.

The next passage widened into a low service corridor with old iron pipes running along the ceiling, greened with age. Moisture dripped steadily into a shallow channel cut into the floor, and the air smelled of damp stone and rust with something faintly sweet underneath, like fruit left too long in heat.

Rachel hated that smell.

It dragged her back to the cages, to heat and voices that had begged right up until they burned.

She nearly missed the ladder bolted to the wall. At the top, a rectangle of faint gray light hovered like a dirty coin.

She grabbed the rungs and climbed.

Halfway up, the pull tightened.

Not hard. Just enough.

Rachel froze, breath caught halfway in, hands slick on the iron. Above her, voices drifted down through the stone, muffled but clear enough to drop her stomach.

Not shouting.
Not panicked.

Working voices.

"...mark the grate. The lower run is compromised."
"Move the cordon. If it comes up here -"
"-- don't touch it bare."

Rachel closed her eyes.

She had not screamed. She had not run through the streets. She had not done anything except bleed and crawl and survive.

They were already here.

The pull tightened again, sharp enough to hurt, and she understood then that it was not telling her up or down.

It was telling her not that way.

Rachel backed down the ladder, rung by careful rung, every faint clink of iron sounding too loud in her ears. Her hands left damp prints behind, shining briefly before the dim swallowed them.

When her boots hit the floor, she turned away from the light.

The pull eased.

"Fine," she mouthed. "Fine."

The corridor split again. Left smelled like sewage and cold air. Right carried a faint trace of smoke and something else she could not place.

Rachel stepped left.

Pain detonated behind her eyes, sudden and punishing, like a nail driven too deep. Her knees buckled and she slammed into the wall, gagging as nausea surged back harder this time, angry, insistent, as if she had dared to argue.

"Stop," she hissed, fury breaking through fear. "Stop doing that."

A word pressed into her skull, not sound but meaning: *No.*

The nausea loosened slowly, like a leash being relaxed one notch.

Rachel stood shaking, staring at the fork. Left was a no. Right was a maybe. That was apparently her life now—a thing inside her deciding where she could go, punishing her when she argued.

*I refuse to accept this.*

But she limped right anyway, because the alternative was curling up on the ground and screaming until her throat gave out, and that wouldn't help anyone.

The air changed as she climbed, cooler and drier, touched by distant wind. She heard a bell then, not the deep alarm horns but something closer, regular, and beneath it the sound of people moving. Not running. Shifting. Being managed.

The passage opened into a low cellar stacked with crates and a door hanging crooked on one hinge. Moonlight spilled through the crack, pale and dirty.

Outside was a narrow back street—shutters barred, paint peeling, the kind of place that stayed quiet on purpose.

Across the way, a woman crouched in a doorway, feeding something small from a cloth sack. A cat. Or a rat. Rachel couldn't tell.

The sight of another living thing that wasn't hunting her made her throat tighten. *A person. An actual person who doesn't know what I am.*

She stepped out before she could think better of it.

Her boot hit the cobbles and the mark on her leg pulsed.

Warmth flooded her blood like a slow pour of whiskey. Her skin prickled. The light under her skin deepened and thickened, as if it had been waiting for open air.

Rachel froze, breathing hard.

The woman looked up.

Her eyes tracked Rachel's face, then her soaked boot, her scraped hands, the soot streaking her clothes, and finally snapped to Rachel's leg.

The woman did not scream.

She just went very still.

Rachel lifted her hands without meaning to.

"I -" she started.

Her tongue went thick. The word slid wrong.

"Don't," Rachel heard herself say, and the sound of her own voice made her flinch.

The woman's mouth opened.

"You -"

Rachel tried again.

"Please," she meant.

"Get back," came out.

The woman lurched to her feet, retreating into the doorway, one hand fumbling behind her for something Rachel did not want to see.

Panic surged, sharp and familiar, and with it came the pressure, the reflex that had already broken rooms and bodies and places that would never be right again.

"No," Rachel rasped. "Wait."

The mark pulsed.

The street vibrated, low and brief, like the stone itself clearing its throat.

The cobbles between Rachel and the woman shifted.

Only a fraction.

Enough.

The woman stumbled as the ground dipped beneath her and slammed back into the doorway hard enough to shake dust loose.

Rachel stared.

"That wasn't—" she started, but she knew it was. Whatever was inside her had just shoved a woman into a wall to keep Rachel isolated.

*It doesn't want me talking to people. It doesn't want me getting help.*

The realization landed cold and heavy.

She was a prisoner in her own body, and the thing holding her didn't even need chains.

Rachel stepped back.

The pull loosened.

The woman stared at her with a kind of fear that had nothing to do with knives or thieves. It was the look you gave a storm when you realized it had noticed you.

Down the street, a lantern flared to life.

Then another.

Bootsteps followed, measured and purposeful.

A calm voice carried through the dark. "Hold positions. No contact."

The woman whispered something and made a sign over her chest with shaking fingers.

Rachel backed into the cellar's shadow.

The pull tightened again, urging her down, away from open air and eyes.

She wanted to scream. She wanted to tear her skin open and dig the thing out.

Instead, she turned and ran the only way she was allowed.

Back underground.

Behind her, lantern light slid along the cobbles like a searching finger, and the calm voice followed, softer now, as if speaking to something skittish and dangerous.

"Easy. Easy. We know what you are."

# Chapter 4 — Testing for Voices

Rachel didn't get far underground before the air cooled and the light thinned to a distant glow. The pressure in her chest tugged left, then right—testing, always testing, like it was mapping the space through her.

Each time she drifted toward sound, nausea snapped tight behind her eyes and the pull shoved her back toward quiet.

*It's not protecting me*, she realized. *It's hiding me.*

The distinction mattered, even if she couldn't figure out why yet.

A low corridor opened ahead, its walls pale and fitted tight, seams clean and old. Light leaked from narrow cuts in the stone, flat and even, thinning the shadows until depth felt wrong.

She slowed. The pressure eased. The air hummed, a faint vibration in the floor that she felt in her teeth.

At the far end of the corridor, a man stood with his hands loose at his sides, dark coat hanging straight. He did not raise a weapon. He did not step forward.

"Rachel," he said.

Her name in a stranger's mouth. *How does he know my name?*

She looked behind him. Grooves cut the floor, shallow lines that crossed like old scars.

"Do not turn around," he said. "They are closing the route behind you."

Rachel took a step back anyway, because she was so goddamn tired of being told what to do. The floor tipped under her heel, just enough to steal her balance. Her vision slid sideways. She froze. The floor leveled.

"We keep records," the man added, answering the question she hadn't asked.

*Of course they do*, Rachel thought bitterly. *I'm something that happened to them. A problem to be documented.*

Heat flared at the mark on her leg. She hissed and shifted her weight, breath catching as the sensation climbed and then settled into a deep, heavy throb.

The man watched her leg. "It does not protect you. It keeps you from being seen."

Behind him, voices carried through stone. Not shouting. Working. "Move the cordon." "Seal the upper run." "No contact."

The pressure tightened in Rachel's chest. She fought it, then stopped. The nausea eased.

"If I go past you?" she asked.

"We burn this corridor," he said. "If you stop, we close it. If you run toward the streets, we cut you off."

Rachel swallowed. "So either way, you keep me here."

"We keep you from making it worse," he said.

The vibration deepened under her boots. The grooves between them hummed in answer, and she understood that the corridor itself could tilt and fold.

The man stepped aside, just enough to open a narrow path between the grooves.

"Follow the line," he said. "Do not touch anyone. Do not call out."

Rachel moved because the pressure nudged her forward and because the stone behind her began to warm.

Behind her, the corridor breathed, and the sound of it closing in was soft and final.

The groove in the floor guided her forward, a shallow track worn smooth by other boots. The pressure stayed low, only tightening when she drifted toward side passages that carried voices.

A door opened ahead. A woman's voice said, "This way. Keep moving." A boy answered, too close.

Rachel stopped. The pressure tightened. Her stomach rolled.

The woman and boy appeared at the far end, guided by a guard who pointed them along and then retreated without looking at Rachel. The woman saw Rachel and froze. The boy peered around her with open curiosity.

"Stay back," Rachel said, and the words came out flat and strange, like something else was speaking through her.

The boy took a step anyway.

The pressure spiked hard. Nausea clawed up her throat. The stone beneath the boy shifted, a small buckle that tipped him forward—

"No!" Rachel screamed, but it didn't matter, the woman was already lunging, her hand brushing Rachel's sleeve—

The corridor shuddered. Air compressed. The woman slammed into the wall with a crack. The boy hit the floor and didn't get up.

Rachel staggered back, hands lifted, vision swimming. "I didn't—I tried to warn them—I didn't want—"

But the words meant nothing. She'd just hurt a child and his mother because the thing inside her didn't want to be touched.

"Clear the lane," a voice called from behind the woman. Boots pounded, measured, not panicked.

Two people in dark coats arrived and went straight to the fallen pair. A third stopped at the groove, eyes on Rachel.

"No contact," he said.

Behind Rachel, stone ground against stone. The corridor she had come through began to seal, seams dimming as if rubbed out.

The pressure tightened and pulled her forward. She stepped because the only other option was the closing wall at her back, because she was a coward, because she couldn't fix what she'd done.

As she limped past, the boy coughed, wet and ragged. The woman groaned, arm at a wrong angle.

*I'm sorry*, Rachel thought, even though thinking it changed nothing. *I'm so sorry.*

The corridor swallowed the sound of their breathing. The line in the floor pulled her on, and she hated herself for following.

# Chapter 5 — The Line Led Downward

The line led her downward, and Rachel followed because the alternative was pain, and she was too exhausted to keep fighting something that lived inside her own chest.

*When did I give up?* she wondered. *When did 'I won't' become 'I can't'?*

The air cooled. The light thinned to seams in the stone that glowed faintly like embers under ash.

Above her, work sounds—voices measured and low, boots stopping and starting, the scrape of wood dragged across stone. A city going about its business while she was herded through its underbelly like something shameful.

A side passage opened to her left, barred with iron. Through it she saw men with lanterns placing a painted cloth across an alley mouth. A woman with a broom stopped, listened, then turned away when a guard touched her shoulder.

Rachel kept moving. The pressure tightened when she paused.

Another junction. Three marks on the stone, painted in dull white. One crossed through. One smeared. One intact. The shapes were not letters she knew, but the meaning pushed in anyway. Her stomach rolled when she looked at the crossed mark. The pressure loosened at the intact one.

She took that path.

The corridor narrowed, then widened into a long service run. Overhead, a grate rattled as a cart rolled across it. Voices carried down.

"Outsider status confirmed."
"Binding is active."
"Keep the lower run clear. No public exposure."

A muffled cry came from somewhere farther down the run, cut short. The pressure shoved her sideways before she could turn toward it.

She stumbled, caught herself on the wall, and kept moving.

The line in the floor reappeared and led her to a hatch. The pressure eased when she touched it.

She pushed.

The hatch resisted, metal warped and stiff, until she leaned her shoulder into it and it scraped open with a sound that felt too loud.

She pulled herself into a narrow courtyard. The air smelled strange—smoke without heat. The pressure loosened.

Above the walls, a slice of sky.

Rachel froze.

The stars were wrong. All wrong, arranged in patterns she'd never seen, clustered too densely near the horizon. And above them—a pale ring cutting across the darkness like someone had drawn a circle around the moon except there was no moon, just that impossible arc of light.

*That's not my sky.*

The thought landed with the weight of a dropped stone. She'd been clinging to the hope that this was some kind of dream, some kind of hallucination, something that would end. But dreams didn't have unfamiliar constellations. Hallucinations didn't rearrange the stars.

She wasn't home. She wasn't anywhere near home. She might not even be on the same fucking planet.

Rachel stood there staring at that wrong sky until her lungs reminded her to breathe, and then she swallowed hard and kept moving, because what else was there to do?

The street beyond was empty by design, shutters drawn, doors marked with a simple line in dull white. A man at the far end carried a crate, saw her, and turned away without a word.

Rachel limped forward. The pressure tightened when she angled toward the brighter street to her right, where voices and market noise carried on a steady hum. Nausea bit hard behind her eyes. She veered back to the quiet.

A calm voice drifted from somewhere nearby. The words were not hers, but she understood them anyway. "Hold positions. No contact."

She saw them then, not as a line of soldiers but as shapes at doorways, people who did not look at her but did not leave. The city was pretending she was not there while it watched her pass.

At the edge where the quiet street met the busy one, the pressure coiled tight. She lifted her foot toward the noise. Pain flashed behind her eyes, and she staggered back.

"Fine," she muttered, breath ragged.

A narrow lane opened to her left, the arch at its end low and dark. The pressure eased as she turned toward it.

Rachel went in because it was the only direction that did not hurt.

# Chapter 6 — The Passage Curved

The passage behind the arch curved inward and down, ceiling low enough to hunch. Damp stone, standing water, and something faintly metallic underneath.

It opened into a small room cut cleanly from stone. Grooves ran floor to ceiling. A bench against one wall. A basin in the corner. And a dark metal frame bolted to the floor—too tall, too narrow, wrong in a way that made her want to look anywhere else.

Rachel stopped just inside.

*A cell*, she thought. *They're putting me in a cell.*

The pressure settled, heavy and still.

The stone panel slid shut behind her without a sound.

She spun and crossed the room toward it, reaching for the seam—and the pressure tightened just enough to stop her hand inches away, like running into glass.

"Let me out," she said, and her voice cracked. "Please. *Please.*"

Nothing answered. The panel stayed sealed.

She stepped back. The pressure eased, satisfied now that she'd learned her place.

A voice came through the stone, low and even. "Remain inside. You will be assessed."

Rachel opened her mouth. The pressure tightened at her throat. The word stuck.

She sat on the bench, hands braced on either side, leg throbbing. The room did not change. The light did not flicker. The only sound was her breathing and the slow drip of water.

A slot opened near the floor. A folded cloth and a small loaf slid through, followed by a shallow bowl of water that settled without sloshing.

Rachel stared at it. The pressure stayed neutral.

She drank, ate a few bites, and waited because waiting was the only thing the room allowed.

Time lost its edges. When the panel finally slid open again, she did not know if it had been minutes or hours.

Footsteps came in pairs. Slow, even. Two people entered. One carried a bundle. The other watched the corners of the room, not Rachel.

"Sit," the woman said.

The pressure loosened a fraction. Rachel lowered herself onto the bench, jaw tight.

The woman cut away the blood-stiff fabric and cleaned the wound. The sting was sharp and clean. Rachel focused on breathing. The pressure stayed low.

"Name," the man said.

The word came through in sounds she'd never heard, syllables that shouldn't have meant anything—but meaning arrived anyway, settling into her brain like it had always belonged there.

*That's not possible*, she thought, even as she tried to answer.

The pressure tightened. The word caught. She shook her head once.

He marked something on his slate anyway.

When the bandage was secured, the woman pressed a dark stamp against Rachel's calf, a mark that left faint heat behind. The pressure twitched.

"Do not touch the frame," the man said. "Do not step toward it if the pull tightens."

Rachel did not answer.

They left as quickly as they had come. The stone panel slid shut.

The pressure shifted toward the frame, a sideways insistence. Rachel stood, took one step, and felt nausea rise.

She stepped back. The pressure eased, as if satisfied to have made its point.

# Chapter 7 — The Room Did Not Stay Still

The room didn't stay still.

Light in the slits thinned, brightened. The pressure in Rachel's chest tightened—not in warning this time. Expectation, like something was about to happen and it knew before she did.

The panel slid open. The same two returned, this time with a narrow case. Behind them, two more stood at the threshold, hands empty, eyes fixed on the floor.

"Stand at the frame," the man said.

*No*, Rachel wanted to say. *Tell me what you're doing first. Tell me what this is for.*

But the pressure nudged her forward, and she moved because she'd learned what happened when she didn't. She stopped at the metal, close enough to feel its cold radiating through her clothes.

*What is this thing? What are they going to do to me?*

The woman opened the case and lifted out a small device. A thin line of light ran along its edge. It hummed when she brought it near the frame.

The pressure went still.

Rachel felt the absence immediately. The device flickered. The woman frowned and tapped it.

Pain flared through Rachel's leg. She hissed and shifted her weight. The frame creaked, a low metal groan that did not belong to the room.

The light along the device guttered. The hum broke.

The frame twisted.

Bolts shrieked as they tore free from the stone. The structure folded inward and collapsed with a heavy clatter.

Rachel staggered back. The pressure slammed into her from the side, forcing her to curl inward. The stone beneath the frame cracked in thin lines that spread a handspan and stopped.

"That is not possible," someone said, and Rachel caught the fear under it.

*What did I do? I didn't do anything. I just stood there.*

The man at the threshold made a small gesture. "Move her."

A door opposite the sealed panel opened. A narrow corridor waited beyond, light too clean to trust.

*Move her*, like she was a package. Like she wasn't standing right here, terrified and confused and *human*.

The pressure pulled her toward it. She went because staying meant the room that had failed, and because fighting had stopped working hours ago.

The corridor sloped downward, narrow and close, walls brushing her shoulders when she drifted. The air smelled clean in a way that made her skin prickle.

The pressure stayed steady, heavier than before, aligned with the corridor as if it had decided there was only one safe direction left.

The floor dipped into a shallow channel lined with smooth stone. Metal rails ran along the walls at shoulder height, polished dull where hands had gripped them.

Rachel stopped at the edge.

The pressure nudged her forward. She stepped in, caught the rail, and the stone beneath her boots vibrated, low and steady.

The channel began to move.

The walls slid past in a steady glide. Openings passed along the sides, narrow alcoves too quick to see into. Once, she caught a glimpse of a face, pale and still, eyes open and unfocused, gone in a blink.

Her leg shook with the effort of balancing. The bandage pulled tight. She held on.

The vibration softened. The channel slowed. The pressure shifted ahead.

Rachel stepped out into a wider corridor, noise bleeding in from beyond. Voices overlapped in uneven bands. Metal struck stone.

Behind her, the channel sealed without sound, leaving nothing but smooth wall.

She followed the pull toward the noise because there was no other direction left.

# Chapter 8 — Released Into Noise

The corridor released her into noise—not loud, not sharp, but layered and uneven. Voices crossing each other in bands that didn't line up cleanly. Metal striking stone somewhere beyond her sight. Footsteps overlapping in rhythms that belonged to different purposes and never quite resolved.

Rachel stepped forward and felt the pressure in her chest loosen, its pull easing into something quieter and more watchful.

*It's letting me breathe*, she thought. *Why is it letting me breathe?*

The answer came immediately: because this was public. Because people were watching. Because whatever lived inside her didn't want to be noticed any more than she did.

The space opened into a wide hall cut deep into the stone, ceiling swallowed by shadow above a grid of hanging lights that burned steady and white. People moved through it in established paths—alone, in pairs, small groups—their trajectories intersecting without pause. No negotiation. No hesitation. A machine made of bodies.

No one stopped when she appeared.

Rachel lingered at the edge with one hand brushing the wall behind her. Someone passed close enough to drag air against her sleeve, carrying the smell of oil and sweat and hot metal. Then another. Then another.

They were ignoring her on purpose. The realization settled like cold water.

*They know what I am. They're pretending I'm not here.*

Tables lined one side of the hall, long and narrow and scarred by use, each occupied by two people facing one another with heads bent and hands busy over slates, papers, tools she could not make out from where she stood. On the opposite side, rails guided a slow-moving line of carts carrying crates stamped with the same simple mark she had seen painted on doors and cloth, workers stepping on and off without breaking stride, loading and unloading with a rhythm that suggested they had done this too many times to count.

Rachel limped forward, her movement immediately noticeable to herself even if no one else reacted, the pressure in her chest stirring when she slowed and easing again when she kept moving, nudging rather than forcing, keeping her just shy of stopping.

A woman passed close enough to glance at Rachel's leg, her eyes flicking to the bandage and then away again without comment, her pace never changing, her broom of attention already sweeping forward to the next thing that needed doing.

Rachel crossed the hall with her steps swallowed by the movement around her. Upright. Exposed. One figure among many, nothing marking her as separate except the limp she couldn't hide and the thing coiled under her ribs.

*I must look insane. Blood-crusted. Terrified. And they're all just... walking past.*

A narrow table stood ahead, empty.

She stopped there because the pressure settled when she did, and because she'd learned to read its preferences. This was where she was supposed to be. This was the next station in whatever processing line she'd been fed into.

A man approached from the far side carrying a slate under one arm and set it down between them, his gaze moving over her face, her posture, the way her weight kept drifting off-center before settling again.

"Name," he said.

Foreign sounds, foreign shapes—and yet meaning poured through anyway, bypassing her ears entirely and landing somewhere deeper. She was starting to get used to it, and that terrified her more than the strangeness itself.

Rachel opened her mouth and felt the pressure tighten, sudden and firm, her throat closing around the word.

The man waited without repeating himself.

She swallowed and forced the sound out, felt it scrape free.

"Rachel."

Her own name. The last thing she had that still pointed at whoever she'd been before all this.

The pressure loosened.

The man marked something on the slate and asked if she could walk. His tone hadn't changed. Rachel shifted her weight—pain flared hot and sharp, then sank back into a dull pull—and said yes.

He made another mark.

"Companions?" he asked.

The question hit somewhere soft and unprotected.

*Companions.* Like she might have brought friends along on this nightmare. Like there might be other people from her world, trapped here, as confused and terrified as she was. The hope hurt more than the injury.

"No," she said, because anything else felt impossible. "I'm alone."

She'd never meant anything more literally in her life.

The slate scraped softly as he wrote.

He stepped aside and lifted one hand, two fingers extended in a small, precise gesture, and someone moved into place beside Rachel, close enough that she could feel their presence without turning her head.

"Follow the line," the man said.

Rachel looked down and saw the shallow groove cut into the stone floor, running from the table toward a series of archways at the far end of the hall, and when she stepped onto it the pressure nudged her forward, subtle but insistent.

Three steps in, she felt it again, the familiar sideways pull under her ribs that angled her attention away from the groove and toward one of the archways to her left, faint at first and then sharper, enough that her pace faltered before she caught herself.

The pressure sharpened.

She corrected, stepping fully back into the groove, breath hitching as the pull eased, and kept walking, aware now of the way the hall had shifted around her without actually stopping.

Someone looked up.

A woman at the nearest table lifted her head, her eyes tracking Rachel's hesitation before dropping to the groove beneath her feet, her hand pausing on the slate just long enough to matter.

Rachel took another step and felt the pull return, stronger this time, her chest tightening as if her lungs had slid slightly off-center, and when she faltered again the groove vibrated beneath her boots, a low sound moving through the stone that carried farther than it should have.

"Stop," the man said.

Rachel froze with the pressure coiled tight and watchful inside her, no longer exploratory, no longer gentle.

The man's eyes moved from her feet to the archway she had drifted toward and back to the slate, his marks quicker now, sharper, and another person stepped closer at her side, their presence firm enough that she felt bracketed without being touched.

"Stay on the line," the man said.

Rachel nodded and stepped back into the groove, and although the pressure loosened again it did not settle the way it had before, its weight uneven, unresolved.

As she walked, she felt eyes on her now, brief glances that slid away too quickly to catch, hands pausing and resuming, paths adjusting just enough to give her space without acknowledging why.

Rachel reached the archway and passed through, the noise dimming behind her as the pressure shifted again, heavier now and focused, its pull no longer curious.

Expectant.

She did not look back.

The corridor narrowed as she walked, not abruptly but in small degrees, the walls easing closer until her shoulders brushed stone whenever she drifted off center. The light thinned into long pale strips that stretched ahead of her and faded behind, and through it all the pressure stayed steady, heavier than before but calm, aligned with the corridor as if it had measured her stride and decided what she could still manage.

She followed because nothing in her body argued against it.

The archway ahead opened into a smaller chamber where the corridor ended cleanly, the stone cut smooth and precise. A shallow circle was scored into the floor, the groove she had been following stopping exactly at its edge. When she stepped into the circle, the pressure eased in a way that felt finished, not permissive, as if the space had been waiting for her and was now satisfied.

Rachel stopped.

The chamber held its shape around her, quiet and contained, the kind of place that expected waiting without needing to enforce it. Two people entered from the opposite side, their footsteps measured, their presence sliding into the space without disturbing it.

One carried a narrow case. The other held a slate pressed flat against their chest. Neither hurried.

Rachel stood in the circle and waited for the pressure to change.

It did not.

The one with the slate stopped just outside the scored line and looked down, then up at Rachel, then back again, their eyes moving with quiet efficiency over information she could not see. The other knelt and set the case down, opening it with practiced motions that did not waste time but did not rush either.

"Hold still," the slate holder said.

Rachel did not answer. She did not need to.

The pressure tightened briefly, then settled again, acknowledging the instruction and allowing it. The person with the case lifted out a small device, its surface dull and worn, a thin line of light running along one edge. They stepped closer without crossing the circle and raised it toward Rachel's chest.

The device hummed, low and even.

The sensation spread outward from her sternum in a shallow wave that made her teeth ache and her vision blur at the edges. Rachel clenched her jaw and stayed upright, fingers flexing uselessly at her sides as the hum shifted pitch and then leveled out again.

The pressure eased.

The slate holder glanced at the device, then marked something down.

"That's stable," they said.

The word landed and stayed.

Rachel let out a breath she had not realized she was holding. The device was lowered, the hum fading as if it had never been there, and the pressure remained low and even, satisfied in a way it had not been before.

The slate holder stepped closer to the circle's edge.

"We're going to move you," they said. "This will be brief."

Rachel nodded. The pressure did not object.

The device came up again, this time angled toward her injured leg. The light along its edge brightened as it drew closer, and for a moment everything stayed aligned, the pressure steady, the chamber quiet.

Then the pressure stopped responding.

Not tightening. Not easing.

It went still, the way air does when someone holds their breath without meaning to. Rachel felt it immediately, the absence more noticeable than any movement, and at the same time the device dipped in the handler's grip.

They frowned and adjusted their hold, tapping the casing once with their thumb.

The light flickered.

Pain flared through Rachel's leg, sharp and immediate, tearing through the bandage and up into her hip. She hissed and shifted her weight without meaning to, her foot sliding a fraction across the stone.

The circle did not react.

The device emitted a short, broken tone.

"That's not right," the slate holder said, their voice quieter now.

The light along the device's edge went out.

For a heartbeat, nothing happened.

Then the floor beneath Rachel's feet shuddered, not the low steady vibration she had felt before but a quick uneven jolt that ran through the stone and up her spine, knocking the breath from her lungs. She staggered, arms lifting instinctively as the circle cracked beneath her, a thin fracture racing along the scored line with a sharp, dry sound.

The person with the device stumbled backward, catching themselves against the wall. The slate holder stepped away from the circle, eyes flicking from the spreading crack to Rachel and back again.

"Back," they said, sharper now.

Rachel tried to move.

Her foot caught.

The pressure did not correct her.

She went down hard, her shoulder striking stone as pain ripped through her leg and chest at once, white flashing across her vision. Someone shouted. The device clattered across the floor and skidded to a stop near the wall, its casing split, the light dark.

Rachel lay on her side, gasping, the room tilting as if it had shifted without telling her. The crack in the floor widened by a hair and then stopped, the stone holding in a way that felt temporary rather than fixed.

"That's impossible," someone said quietly.

The pressure returned.

Not smoothly. Not aligned.

It pressed in from the side, heavy and urgent, forcing Rachel to curl inward, to protect what it could without regard for what broke in the process. The vibration faded. The crack stopped spreading.

Rachel lay shaking, her breath ragged, her body folded awkwardly against the stone.

Around her, the chamber filled with sound - voices overlapping, footsteps rushing in from the corridor, orders that did not quite settle into agreement. Hands reached for her, then hesitated.

The pressure flared once, hard and abrupt, and someone yelped as they were shoved sideways by nothing Rachel could see.

Then it was gone again.

Rachel stayed where she was, staring at the fractured circle beneath her, the broken device near the wall, the faces above her tight and uncertain.

No one spoke the word.

No one had to.

This was no longer routine.

# Chapter 9 — Not Lifted Gently

They did not lift her gently.

They lifted her like a problem that had to be moved before it got worse, hands under her shoulders and hips and then a shift of weight that tore a sound out of her throat when her bandaged leg dragged across the stone. Rachel tried to help out of reflex, tried to make herself smaller and easier, but her body did not have the coordination for it, pain and shock eating the space between thought and motion.

"Keep her inside the circle," someone said, and another voice answered, "The circle's compromised."

They moved anyway.

The fractured line in the floor sat under her like a broken boundary, the crack widened just enough to make the scored shape look wrong, like it had been drawn and corrected too many times. Rachel saw it in flashes through the white glare in her vision, the faces above her slipping in and out of focus, mouths moving too fast for her brain to hold the words.

Something brushed her ribs from the inside.

The pressure.

It was not pulling now. It was braced, like an elbow jammed into a door.

They carried her out through the corridor, the air changing as they crossed thresholds, the light shifting from thin and clinical to warmer and dirtier, noise rising in layers ahead. Somewhere behind them, someone was talking quickly, listing steps, counting failures.

"...device is dead. Circle line is-"
"-don't say it like that."
"-it is compromised-"
"-move."

The hall came back at her in a blur, the smell of oil and sweat and stone dust, the constant movement of people who did not stop just because one thing had gone wrong. Heads turned as she passed, not gawking, not dramatic, quick glances that snapped back to work.

Rachel's leg throbbed with each jolt of the carry. The bandage felt too tight now, like the swelling had decided to catch up all at once, and her stomach rolled hard enough that she made a low sound and someone swore.

"Set her down," a voice said, and then, "No - keep moving."

They did not set her down.

They angled away from the tables and the groove, away from the visible lines that told the hall how to behave, and Rachel felt the pressure shift against her ribs as if it noticed the change too, the same sideways lean that had gotten her caught before.

A door appeared in the stone wall where she had not noticed one earlier, darker than the surrounding rock, its edges cut clean. They pushed through it without slowing.

Inside was quieter.

Not safe quiet.

Work quiet.

The room was narrow and bright, lit from slits high above, the floor stained in pale ghosts of older spills. A bench ran along one wall. Along the other stood a table with straps folded on top of it as if they had been set down between uses.

Rachel's throat tightened.

They laid her on the bench instead of the table, not because they were kind but because her leg would not tolerate the height without her screaming, and screaming drew attention in this place the way blood drew flies.

Someone pressed a hand to her shoulder. "Look at me."

Rachel tried.

A face hovered close, sharp eyes, calm mouth. Calm in a way that felt practiced.

"Do you understand what I'm saying?"

Rachel nodded. The motion made her vision spark.

The person glanced over their shoulder. "Sedate her lightly. Just enough to keep her still."

Rachel's heart jumped.

"No," she said, or tried to. It came out thin.

The pressure pinched once, quick and unpleasant.

Someone approached with a vial and a cloth. Rachel turned her head, tried to pull away, but hands caught her forearm and wrist, firm and controlled.

"Just a little," the calm voice said. "We're not hurting you."

The words did not match the room. They did not match the straps waiting nearby.

Rachel inhaled and the cloth pressed to her face smelled sharply of something herbal and chemical at the same time. She tried to hold her breath. It did not matter.

The room slid.

Thought softened at the edges. The pressure did not disappear, but it loosened the wrong way, like a rope going slack because the knot had shifted.

Her eyelids grew heavy.

"She's dropping," someone said.

"Good."

Rachel fought to keep her eyes open. Managed it for a second. Then gray washed in.

Her pulse thudded loud and uneven.

Under it, something else.

Not a voice.

A decision.

The pressure returned before she did.

It came in at an angle, not pulling forward or back but up, like something had hooked under her sternum and tried to lift her out of herself. Rachel's eyes snapped open without her choosing it. Her breath tore in.

Hands tightened.

"What-"

The room felt wrong, distances slipping. Rachel gagged against the cloth still near her mouth.

"Hold her," the calm voice said, thinner now.

Someone pressed her shoulder.

The pressure flared sideways.

Not at them. Past them.

The air snapped, dry and sharp, and the person at Rachel's left stumbled as if shoved by an unseen shoulder, catching hard on the table. The folded straps slid, one end dropping to the floor with a slap.

"Stop," someone said.

Rachel lay there breathing too fast, half-awake and wrong.

The person with the vial stared at it as if it had changed.

"It shouldn't-"

"It didn't," another voice cut in.

"Run the read," the calm voice said.

"We don't have a device."

"Use the strip."

A narrow band was pulled from a drawer and brought toward Rachel's chest. As it neared her skin, the pressure went still again, the held-breath absence returning, and the strip's edge curled slightly as if the air had thickened.

The handler hesitated.

"Do it," the calm voice said.

The strip touched Rachel's skin.

For half a second, nothing.

Then the center darkened, blooming inward instead of spreading.

The hand jerked back.

"That's not her," they said, and their voice had lost its neutrality.

Silence shifted.

"Again," the calm voice said.

The strip hovered above Rachel's sternum this time, not touching. The pressure leaned outward.

It darkened anyway.

"That's not possible," someone whispered.

"Move her," the calm voice said.

"Where?"

A pause.

"Off the line."

The words changed the room.

Someone swore softly. Someone else started to object and stopped.

They lifted Rachel again, faster now, not careful with her leg because the priority had changed. She made a sound she could not stop, and the pressure braced hard in her ribs, keeping her from folding.

They moved through a back corridor, light thinner, air colder, sound falling away. Rachel tried to track turns. Left. Right. Down. Her thoughts dragged, sedation still thick.

They passed a doorway and she glimpsed metal frames, scored circles, a rack of devices like broken teeth.

"It's concurrent," one carrier muttered.

"It's not supposed to be."

The corridor opened into a wide channel, stone smooth and worn, rails set at shoulder height. Vibration hummed underfoot.

They set her into it and forced her fingers to the rail.

"Hold."

Rachel's grip tightened.

The channel began to move.

Behind her, the calm voice spoke again, not reassurance or threat, just procedure. "If it spikes, drop her."

Rachel tried to turn her head. The motion of the channel stole it.

The corridor slid past, light stretching and thinning, and Rachel clung to the rail as the pressure in her chest settled heavy and focused, not pulling toward safety or away from danger, just holding her in a line that did not belong to her.

She did not know where they were taking her.

She knew it was worse.

The channel carried her longer than the first time.

Rachel clung to the rail with fingers that did not quite feel like hers, her grip tightening and loosening in small involuntary pulses as the vibration traveled up her arms and settled in her shoulders. The corridor slid past in stretched bands of light and shadow, the air thinning and cooling in a way that made her lungs ache on the inhale, and the pressure in her chest stayed heavy and focused, not pulling her toward safety or away from danger, just keeping her aligned with motion.

Her leg throbbed in a steady, deep rhythm under the bandage, the pain spreading when she shifted her weight and narrowing to a hot point when she held still. She tried to find a position that did not make her want to vomit and failed, and somewhere behind her the words If it spikes, drop her replayed as if her brain had latched onto them for something to hold.

The channel bent.

It descended.

The light thinned.

Rachel's stomach rolled hard and she swallowed against it, eyes fixed forward because looking anywhere else made the moving walls feel too close.

The pressure eased for half a second.

Not relief.

A recalculation.

Then it pressed again, slightly off-center, as if the line she was on was not the one the world preferred.

Rachel tightened her grip and rode it.

The channel slowed.

The vibration softened.

Ahead, the corridor widened into a rough-cut opening that did not match the clean stone she had seen in the processing hall. The edges were uneven, tool marks visible in the rock, and the air that seeped out carried a different smell, damp earth and old smoke, something faintly animal beneath it.

The channel stopped.

Rachel loosened her fingers with effort, her hands tingling as she let go, and stepped out onto stone that felt less finished under her boots, grittier, dust catching in the seams.

Behind her, the channel did not seal smoothly this time.

The stone hesitated, as if deciding whether it was allowed to close at all, and then slid partway, leaving a narrow gap of darkness where it should have been seamless.

Rachel stared at it.

The pressure tightened.

She turned away.

The corridor ahead was narrower and lower than the hallways she had been moved through before, the ceiling dipping in places where the rock bulged, forcing her to duck. Light came from small openings cut into the wall at irregular intervals, dim and yellowed, more like lantern light than the steady white she had grown used to.

No voices.

No footsteps.

No carts.

The silence here was not absence. It was ownership.

Rachel limped forward, one hand skimming the wall for balance. The sedation still dragged at her thoughts, making everything feel a fraction delayed. She tried to count her steps to keep herself present and lost the numbers twice.

The pressure stayed heavy.

It did not tug her forward so much as keep her from stopping, tightening when she hesitated and easing when she moved, a simple brutal agreement, motion in exchange for air.

The corridor opened into a larger space.

Not a hall.

Not a room.

A chamber that looked like it had been repurposed and then repurposed again, stone walls pocked with old bolt holes, stains in irregular arcs where something had once been mounted and removed. The floor dipped toward the center, and there a shallow depression held a dark patch that might have been water or old burn.

A metal frame stood near one wall, not the same as the one she had broken, but close enough that her stomach clenched anyway. Beside it, a rack of objects hung in neat order, thin rods, loops of chain, a curved tool with a blunt edge that caught the dim light.

Rachel stopped without meaning to.

The pressure tightened hard.

She breathed in sharply and felt the air scrape.

Her leg shook.

The chamber was not empty.

A person sat in the far corner on a low stool, elbows on their knees, head bowed as if they had been waiting long enough to get tired of looking up. They wore plain clothing, darker than the stone, and their hair was cut short.

As Rachel stepped forward, the person lifted their head.

Their eyes landed on Rachel and held.

Assessing.

Rachel's mouth dried.

She took another step and the pressure eased by a fraction, enough that she could inhale without her ribs protesting. The relief was small and immediate.

The person in the corner stood.

They moved carefully, like someone with an old injury, and when they stepped into the dim light Rachel saw the bruising on one forearm, the faint marks circling their wrist.

"You're off," the person said.

Rachel stared. "What?"

The person's gaze flicked to Rachel's chest and then away.

"Off the line," they said again. "They don't bring people down here unless they don't fit."

Rachel swallowed. "Where is here?"

The person snorted softly. "A place they pretend isn't part of the system. A place where mistakes go to stop being mistakes."

Rachel's pulse sped.

The pressure in her chest shifted, pressing at an angle that made her want to turn her body slightly.

The person watched that too.

"You feel it," they said.

Rachel's voice came out thin. "Feel what."

The person took a slow step closer and stopped outside arm's reach.

"You're not alone," they said.

It was the first thing in this place that sounded like a promise.

Rachel's stomach tightened.

"Don't say it," the person added. "If you name it, they'll hear it faster."

Rachel's skin prickled.

"Who are you," she said.

The person hesitated.

"Lena," they said. "I've been here long enough that the people upstairs stopped asking what I was before I arrived."

Rachel swallowed. "Why are you helping me."

Lena's mouth tightened. "I'm not. I'm telling you what's true."

Rachel shifted her weight and her leg flared with pain. She hissed and braced herself against the wall.

The pressure eased.

Lena's eyes flicked to Rachel's hand, then to the corridor behind her.

"You're bleeding under that wrap," Lena said. "And you're drugged."

Rachel did not answer.

Lena's gaze moved to the rack of tools.

"They'll come," Lena said. "Not the ones with slates. The other ones."

Rachel's breath shortened. "How do you know."

"Because you're here," Lena said. "And because it already twitched."

Rachel frowned. "What."

Lena's eyes went distant for a heartbeat.

"The air. The pressure. That thing that isn't you."

Rachel felt a small shift under her ribs, not a pull but a tightening in place.

Lena stepped back.

"Do you know what they do when they decide you're concurrent," Lena asked.

Rachel did not answer.

"They cut," Lena said. "They cut until the overlap stops. If it doesn't stop, they cut more. If it still doesn't stop, they stop calling it a person."

Rachel's stomach lurched.

The pressure tightened.

"You have one advantage," Lena said. "They're scared of you. That buys you seconds. Not mercy."

Rachel swallowed. "What do I do."

Lena looked at the channel opening, the rack of tools, then back to Rachel.

"Pick," she said. "You don't get time to understand. You get time to choose what hurts."

A sound came from the corridor behind Rachel.

Stone sliding.

Slow.

The pressure went still.

Held breath.

Rachel turned toward the sound, her leg trembling.

Lena stepped farther back, giving Rachel space.

"Pick," Lena said again. "Because if you don't, it will."

The stone ground again.

The gap widened.

The pressure leaned, just slightly, toward the tools.

# Chapter 10 — The Tools

Rachel did not move toward the tools.
