The pressure leaned that way anyway, subtle at first and then more insistent, the same sideways insistence she had learned to recognize, the promise of motion if she followed and the threat of collapse if she did not, and she stood there with her hand braced against the wall, her leg shaking hard enough now that she could feel it up through her hip.

"Don't," Lena said quietly.

Rachel turned her head before she decided to listen.

Lena stood farther back than before, one shoulder nearly touching the stone, her hands empty and visible at her sides, not looking at the rack or the half-sealed channel gap but at Rachel's face, as if it mattered which way her eyes went next.

"They want you to choose fast," Lena said. "That's how they keep it clean."

The pressure tightened in response, sharp enough that Rachel sucked in a breath and tasted metal.

"They're coming," Rachel said, the words scraping on the way out.

"I know," Lena said. "That's why I'm still here."

The chamber felt smaller now, the walls pressing in by degrees the way the corridors had earlier, and under that was the other pressure, the one that did not care about walls or exits or tools, only outcomes. Rachel looked back at the rack and felt how easy it would be to reach for something, anything, to hurt first and make herself impossible to handle.

The thought slid into place too smoothly.

She hated that.

Rachel turned back to Lena. "If I stay," she said, and her voice shook despite her effort to keep it steady, "does it make it worse for you."

Lena did not answer right away.

The pause stretched just long enough to tell the truth.

"Yes," Lena said finally. "Eventually."

The pressure pressed harder, almost pleased, and Rachel felt it like fingers tightening under her ribs.

She took a step toward Lena.

The pressure flared sharp and angry, no longer nudging but resisting, a sudden tightness that stole half her breath and sent sparks across her vision. Rachel gasped and nearly went down, her hand slapping the wall as her balance gave.

Lena moved without thinking, crossing the space and catching Rachel under the arm before she could slide, the contact brief but solid, human weight and heat anchoring her just long enough to matter.

The pressure recoiled.

Not gone.

Offended.

Rachel leaned into Lena's shoulder, breathing hard, aware of how wrong her body felt, how close she was to folding completely if she let go.

"They don't like that," Lena said.

Rachel let out a broken laugh. "Good."

Behind them, stone ground against stone, louder now, closer, and the gap widened enough that cold air spilled into the chamber, carrying the smell of clean rock and metal.

Lena pulled back but kept a hand on Rachel's arm. "If you trust me," she said, "you don't get to stop halfway."

Rachel met her eyes. "I don't have a halfway."

"That's not what I meant."

Another grinding sound followed, slower and heavier, and a shadow passed across the gap without rushing.

The pressure surged, no longer subtle, a hard shove toward the rack that made Rachel's teeth clack together. She hissed and planted her feet, pain tearing through her leg as she resisted, breath coming in sharp, ugly pulls.

Lena swore. "It's pushing."

"I know," Rachel said. "I can feel it."

Lena's grip tightened. "Then listen to me. Not it."

Rachel nodded once.

Lena turned and tugged her along, not toward the tools and not toward the channel gap but toward the far wall where the stone dipped and the light thinned into deeper shadow, a place Rachel had not really noticed before because she had been watching the obvious exits.

The pressure spiked.

Rachel stumbled and Lena nearly went down with her, both of them catching against the wall as the pressure slammed again, furious now, a sideways crush that made Rachel cry out as something in her chest pulled too hard and too fast.

"Sorry," Lena said through clenched teeth, hauling her upright. "I know it hurts."

"That's new," Rachel gasped.

"No," Lena said. "That's it realizing you're not useful this way."

The words landed wrong and right at the same time.

Behind them, the stone slid open another inch. A shape moved in the gap, tall and narrow, unhurried.

The pressure coiled tight, like a fist held back.

Lena shoved her shoulder into the wall and a section of stone scraped aside, not smoothly or cleanly, just enough to open a narrow recess that bit into Rachel's shoulder as Lena pushed her through first and twisted sideways to follow.

"You didn't say-" Rachel started.

"I said trust me," Lena said. "Not explain."

The pressure lunged.

This time it tore sideways through Rachel's ribs, sharp and disorienting, like being grabbed from the inside and yanked. Her knees buckled.

Lena dragged her anyway.

The recess was barely wide enough for two people, the air damp and rusted, the floor uneven. The pressure slammed again and this time struck stone instead of flesh, the force bleeding outward with a hollow boom that shook dust loose from above.

Something outside shouted.

"Here," Lena said, breathless. "Stay close."

Rachel pressed her back to the wall, lungs burning, and felt the pressure gather again, uncertain now, no longer aligned with an obvious outcome.

For the first time since the basement, it hesitated.

Footsteps sounded outside the recess, careful and measured. Light cut across the narrow opening as something passed in front of it.

"They'll find us," Rachel whispered.

"Yes," Lena said. "But not yet."

Rachel swallowed, her leg throbbing, her chest aching, the pressure hovering and resentful, its lean no longer clear.

She looked at Lena in the dim, at the bruises on her arm, the set of her jaw.

"Why help me," Rachel asked again.

Lena's mouth twitched. "Because you didn't pick the tools."

The footsteps paused.

Rachel held her breath.

The pressure pressed lightly outward, testing, as if it were deciding whether this trust was something it could still use.

The pressure did not push anymore.

Rachel noticed it because her body kept bracing for it anyway, muscles tight and waiting for the shove that never came, the absence sitting wrong in her chest like the moment after a sound cuts out and your ears keep reaching for it.

Footsteps passed the opening again, close enough that light slid across the narrow gap and touched the stone at Rachel's feet before pulling away. She held her breath without deciding to and felt how little air she actually needed when fear did the work.

Lena stayed still beside her, shoulder pressed into the wall, eyes on the opening. She did not look at Rachel. She did not need to.

Something shifted.

Not outside.

Inside.

Rachel's stomach clenched hard enough to make her gag silently, her hand flying to her mouth as nausea surged up fast and sharp, not the slow roll of pain or panic but a sudden rearranging, like her body had been grabbed and twisted a fraction out of place. She swallowed it back and tasted copper, thick and immediate.

Lena glanced at her then, her expression tightening. "That shouldn't be happening."

Rachel nodded, eyes watering. "It's-" She stopped as the sensation deepened, spreading under her ribs and down into her gut, not pain exactly but pressure without direction, like something settling where it did not belong. She sagged against the wall and made a low sound she did not recognize as her own.

Outside, someone spoke.

Not loud.

"...feel that?"

Another voice answered, quieter. "Yes."

Rachel's heart kicked.

Whatever was inside her went still.

The footsteps resumed, slower now, closer to the opening, and Rachel felt the pressure tilt, not toward the sound but sideways, brushing against Lena's presence in a way that made Rachel's chest tighten.

Lena stiffened. Her jaw set. "No," she said under her breath. "Don't."

The pressure squeezed.

Rachel cried out, sharp and involuntary, as heat flared under her skin and raced along her arms and across her back, too fast to follow. Her hands curled into fists without asking her, nails biting into her palms as the air around her thinned with a faint snapping sound, brittle and wrong.

Outside, the voices sharpened.

"There," someone said. "Again."

Rachel's vision swam. The recess felt smaller, the stone closer, and her breath came in short panicked pulls as her body reacted faster than her thoughts could keep up.

Lena grabbed her wrist. "It's doing something to you."

"I know," Rachel said, gasping. "I didn't-"

"I know," Lena said again, harder this time.

The pressure pulsed, warmer now, heavier, and Rachel felt a strange loosening under her sternum, like a knot easing without permission. The sensation spread, unpleasant and almost relieving at the same time, and she hated it for that.

Outside, light held steady.

A shape blocked the opening.

Rachel squeezed her eyes shut and the pressure eased just enough to let her think, the sudden shift making her dizzy.

"They're going to open it," Lena said.

Rachel swallowed, throat dry. The pressure gathered again, not sharp now but dense, humming faintly in her bones. Her skin prickled. Her pulse thundered.

Lena's grip tightened. "Look at me."

Rachel forced her eyes open.

"If you let it keep going," Lena said, voice tight, "they won't need the tools."

Rachel shook her head weakly. "What happens."

Lena hesitated.

"That depends," she said slowly, "on how much of you it wants to show them."

Stone scraped outside.

The wall shuddered as something heavy pressed against it, testing the fit.

Rachel slid down the wall until she was sitting on the uneven floor, breath tearing in and out of her chest, her head bowed. The pressure swelled inside her, not pushing her anywhere, just building, as if it were waiting for her to stop resisting long enough to finish whatever it had started.

Her thoughts scattered.

The tools flashed in her mind. The channel. The way the pressure had turned toward Lena like it had noticed her for the first time.

Rachel pressed her back harder into the stone until the ache grounded her, sharp and familiar, something she could name.

The pressure recoiled a fraction.

Outside, someone cursed softly.

The stone shifted again, slower now, uncertain.

Rachel stayed where she was, shaking, every muscle tight, the pressure coiled inside her no longer trying to move her hands or her feet.

Just waiting.

# Chapter 11 — The Footsteps Did Not Rush

The footsteps did not rush, and that was the first wrong thing.

Rachel felt it before she understood it, the way the air outside the recess changed, the way the quiet settled instead of breaking, like the stone itself had made a decision and was just waiting for the people to catch up. Her shoulders stayed tight, her muscles braced for noise or impact or shouting, and when none of that came it left her stranded in the waiting, listening too hard.

Light slid across the opening again. Not a sweep. A placement. Deliberate, steady, like someone had leaned in and decided this was the angle they wanted.

Lena's hand stayed on Rachel's wrist. Not squeezing. Just there. A point of contact that felt almost unreal compared to the pressure coiled under Rachel's ribs, heavy and contained, like something holding its breath with her.

Rachel kept her back pressed to the stone and breathed shallow through her nose, counting without numbers. The pressure stayed quiet. Not gone. Quiet in a way that made her skin itch.

A voice spoke from outside.

Not the calm one from before. This one was flatter, older, worn smooth by repetition.

"Subject located."

Another voice answered, closer. "Confirm."

A pause stretched, long enough that Rachel felt her stomach tighten around it.

"Concurrent response observed."

The words slid into her chest and sat there, ugly and heavy.

Lena's jaw set. She did not look at Rachel. She watched the opening like she was measuring how much space it would take to ruin everything.

"Open," the flat voice said.

Stone scraped.

The wall shuddered as something leaned into it, not forcing, just insisting, patient pressure that made the seam begin to move. Cold air spilled into the recess carrying the smell of metal and clean rock, the kind of clean that did not mean safe.

The pressure inside Rachel flared, quick and hot, and she bit down hard, keeping the sound locked in her chest where it burned.

"There," someone said.

The opening widened another inch.

Boots came into view, careful and placed, and Rachel hated that more than if they had rushed. The chamber beyond looked wrong now, too orderly, the tools hanging neat and patient in the dim light like they had been waiting for permission.

Lena shifted without looking at her, stepping slightly in front, shoulder angling into the space. Rachel saw the tremor in her arm and thought, stupidly, she is tired, even though she knew that was not it.

"Step out," the flat voice said.

No threat. No explanation.

Rachel swallowed. Her mouth opened before her brain caught up. "We can't-"

"Rachel."

Lena said her name low and urgent, like a hand closing around something fragile. "Listen."

Rachel turned her head just enough to see her, the hard line of her jaw, the way she was not blinking.

"They're not here for both of us," Lena said. "They won't risk it."

"I'm not leaving you," Rachel said, because the words were already there and because saying nothing felt worse.

Lena breathed out through her nose, slow and controlled. "You already did. The moment you came in here."

The pressure tightened then, not sharp, just heavy, like something shifting its weight, and Rachel felt how much space it took up when it moved, how it crowded everything else out without making a sound.

Outside, the voice repeated, "Step out."

A softer sound followed. Metal against metal. Something being prepared without ceremony.

Lena's fingers tightened on Rachel's wrist. "If they see you," she said, "they'll take you. And it won't stop where it did last time."

Rachel's breath caught. "Where did it-"

Lena did not answer.

The opening widened just enough for a hand to reach through.

A gloved hand appeared, fingers spread, palm angled inward toward the darkness, patient and still.

The pressure inside Rachel twitched, sharp and responsive, like it recognized the shape of the gesture even if she did not.

"Don't," Lena said. Not to Rachel.

The hand paused.

For a moment everything held - breath, light, stone - like the world had forgotten what came next.

Then the flat voice said, "Secondary present."

Rachel froze.

Lena went very still.

The pressure inside Rachel quieted in a way that felt wrong, like something lowering itself just out of sight.

Another voice cut in, sharper. "Bring the secondary."

"No," the flat voice said immediately. "Not both."

A pause followed, long enough for Rachel to hear the measured breathing outside, the scrape of fabric against stone.

"Contain the secondary," the sharp voice said.

"Isolate," the flat voice corrected.

Lena exhaled once, slow. "There," she whispered. "That's it."

Rachel stared at her. "What are you-"

Lena moved.

She pulled Rachel deeper into the recess for a heartbeat and then shoved her sideways behind a jut of stone Rachel had not noticed, a narrow bend that broke the line of sight. Rachel's shoulder struck hard and white pain flashed across her vision. She bit down on a cry.

Lena stepped forward into the light.

Rachel reached for her and missed, fingers scraping cloth.

"Lena-"

Lena did not look back.

She lifted her hands, palms out, slow and visible, like she had practiced this in her head a thousand times.

The pressure surged, furious now, hot and insistent, and Rachel pressed her forehead into the stone and breathed through it, refusing to give it movement.

"It's her," Lena said evenly. "Not me."

"You were with her," the sharp voice said.

"I was near her," Lena answered.

"State designation," the flat voice said.

Lena hesitated.

Rachel felt it like a pulled thread, the pause stretching too long, tight and dangerous.

"Hold," Lena said.

The word landed with weight. Rachel heard it in the way the air shifted, the way the silence snapped tight around it.

"Hold confirmed," the flat voice replied.

"Move her," the sharp voice said.

Hands closed around Lena's arm. She adjusted her stance instead of fighting, turning with the pull like she knew exactly how resistance would be read, how it would be written down later.

Rachel's chest burned. The pressure leaned toward the opening, eager and bright, like it wanted her to break cover and make everything loud again.

She stayed still.

She counted her breaths until the numbers stopped meaning anything.

Through the bend in the stone, she saw Lena's silhouette turn toward the corridor.

Lena looked back once.

Her eyes found the dark pocket where Rachel was hidden, and there was no comfort in her expression, no promise. Just instruction, hard and simple.

Don't follow.

Then she was gone.

The voices moved with her. The light shifted. The chamber noise thinned as the door swallowed them.

Stone ground.

The recess began to close.

Rachel stared at the narrowing strip of light, at the empty space where Lena had been, and the pressure inside her tightened, not pulling now, not pushing, just compressing like a hand around her heart.

The stone sealed shut.

Rachel was alone in the dark.

She did not scream.

She did not move.

And in the black, the pressure settled, quiet and patient, as if it had been waiting for this shape of absence all along.

Rachel did not scream when the recess sealed. She did not have air to waste on it.

She pressed a hand to the wall where Lena had pushed her and felt for a seam. Smooth. Cold. No give. The pressure inside her tightened, not a shove, a warning.

Lena's last look stayed with her, hard and instructive.

Somewhere deeper in the stone, a hum woke, low and unsteady. A line of light stitched itself across the floor, thin as chalk.

The pressure leaned toward it.

Rachel followed because the only other direction was the wall at her back and the sound of something waking behind it.

The corridor beyond was rougher than the intake halls, old stone, uneven floor. The light lines ran in short segments, guttering. She limped, dragging her bad foot, and every time she slowed the pressure tightened until nausea blurred her vision.

A door ahead slid open without noise. Cold air pushed out. The floor lines brightened.

She stepped through and felt the pressure spike, hard enough to make her gasp.

The room was bare, a square chamber with embedded lines and no exit she could see. The moment she moved, the lines responded, as if tracking her weight.

A voice spoke from nowhere and everywhere at once. Flat. Distant.

"Subject relocated."

Rachel backed toward the door she had come through. The pressure slammed into her chest and stopped her cold. The door sealed without sound.

"Stand by," the voice said.

A seam opened in the floor.

Rachel swore and lunged sideways, but the pressure shoved her the other way. The opening widened, light bleeding up from below like a deep well.

"Transport initiated."

She dropped.

# Chapter 12 — The Chute

Rachel hit the chute hard and slid, the stone tilted just enough that she could not slow herself. Air tore past her ears. The pressure inside her spiked and then flattened, as if it was bracing for impact it could not prevent.

Voices echoed above, distorted by distance and motion.

"No stabilizers."
"She is already in transit."
"Log it."

She slammed into a bend, shoulder first. Pain flashed white behind her eyes. The chute leveled and spit her into a smaller room, and she tumbled onto stone that vibrated under her weight.

Light came on. Clean. Steady.

She pushed herself upright, breath tearing, and pressed her back to the wall. The pressure stayed coiled and watchful under her ribs.

The door across the room hissed open.

Two people entered. Light gear, clean clothing, no obvious weapons. One carried a slate. The other rolled a low cart with thin instruments that looked too delicate to trust.

"Delivered," the slate holder said, glancing down. "Late."

"Within tolerance," the other replied, already pulling gloves on.

Rachel shook her head. "Don't touch me."

The slate holder looked up, not alarmed. "Speak when prompted."

The pressure flickered, sharp and brief, like a held breath.

"Classification?" the other asked.

"Low," the slate holder said without looking at Rachel. "Routine."

The word landed wrong, heavy as a dropped plate.

"No," Rachel said. "That's wrong."

They moved in anyway.

The instant fingers closed around her wrist, the room lurched. Heat ripped through Rachel's arm and chest, a tearing sensation that felt like being pulled apart and shoved back together wrong. She screamed as force burst outward, not precise, not shaped, just there.

The handler flew backward and hit the floor hard. The cart tipped, instruments clattering across stone.

The floor lines flared bright.

No alarm sounded.

The slate holder stared at the fallen body, then at Rachel, then at the slate. "That is not-"

Rachel sagged against the wall, gasping, vision dimming around the edges. The pressure coiled tight inside her, heavy and present, humming faintly.

Footsteps arrived at a run. More than two. The door hissed again.

"We have a misfile," the slate holder said, voice tight now.

Rachel closed her eyes.

Whatever rules had failed to follow her into this room had finally arrived.

They did not rush her. They hovered instead, stopping short of the etched lines as if the floor were a fence.

Someone swore under their breath. Another voice said, "Secondary," and the word chilled the air.

"No contact," a woman said, louder. "Back up."

Boots scraped stone. Space opened around Rachel, too wide, too bright.

The pressure inside her shifted, restless, like it did not like the distance.

A seam split along one wall.

Something spilled out low and fast. Not smoke. Not liquid. It hugged the floor, dense and heavy, swallowing light where it passed.

Cold punched through Rachel's boots, biting and wrong. She tried to scramble back and the pressure slammed down, crushing and sudden, pinning her in place hard enough that her vision went white at the edges.

"No," she gasped.

The dark climbed her calves. Burning and freezing at once.

"Rollback," someone shouted.
"We cannot," another voice answered, high and thin. "It is already engaged."

The etched lines flared.

Rachel clawed at the stone. The pressure inside her surged wild and jagged, heat bursting through her chest in uneven pulses that made everything worse.

Then it shifted.

Not gone. Different.

The heat under her skin steadied, no longer bursting at random. The dark slowed, then hesitated, as if it had met something it could not calculate.

"Hold," the woman said. "Do not touch her."

The cold pulled back an inch. Then another.

Rachel sagged forward, gasping, legs burning as feeling crept back in uneven waves.

The dark drained toward the seam, retreating in ripples until stone ground shut over it again.

Silence hit the room. Not empty. Loaded.

"Clear the sector," the woman said.

"We cannot leave her-"
"We cannot keep her," another voice snapped.

Rachel lay on her side, shaking, listening to boots retreat, to equipment being rolled back, to the door sealing.

She needed a way out before they decided to cut her.

The lights dimmed. The etched lines dulled.

She was alone again, but the air was no longer calm. It hummed with something that had been woken and did not want to go back to sleep.

# Chapter 13 — The Room Started Emptying

The room started emptying before Rachel could get her breath back.

No announcements. No sirens. People backed out in quick, controlled steps, equipment lifted and rolled away, voices dropped to the edges of hearing. The lights thinned with every person who left.

The pressure inside her tracked it all, tightening, easing, tightening again, like it was keeping count.

"Move," a man said from the doorway, not looking at her. "You cannot stay in this chamber."

Rachel laughed once, raw and tired. "You just said clear the sector."

"This is not the sector anymore," he said, and that did not make sense until the floor lines shifted.

A seam opened near the far wall. Cold air breathed out.

The pressure leaned toward it.

Rachel pushed herself up, slow and shaking, and limped across the room before the floor decided for her. Her leg screamed. She bit down on it and kept moving.

The doorway behind her sealed as she crossed the threshold.

The corridor beyond was narrow, older, rough stone under her boots. The light lines here were thinner and less certain, as if they had not been used in a long time.

Voices carried from a junction ahead. Two, maybe three. The pressure snapped tight and shoved her sideways into a darker passage.

She did not argue. She did not have breath to waste.

The side passage dipped and tightened. Pipes ran along the ceiling, damp and rusted. A grate on her right opened into a lower run where a pair of workers hurried past, glancing up without seeing her.

Rachel took one step toward the grate. She opened her mouth to warn them, and no sound came out the way she meant it. A cold refusal pinched her throat closed.

Nausea knifed through her. The pressure locked her knees and she sagged against the wall until it eased.

The workers kept moving. One of them laughed, tired, and the sound hurt.

Rachel turned away and followed the narrow run downward as the corridor behind her sealed with a soft, final grind.

The maintenance run smelled of damp metal and old smoke. Pipes sweated along the ceiling. The floor slanted just enough to keep her leg screaming.

Rachel followed the faintest line of light, the pressure bracketing her, tightening when she slowed, easing when she moved.

Ahead, a door stood ajar. Light spilled through it. Voices, close.

She took one step toward it.

Pain flashed behind her eyes. Her knees buckled. The pressure slammed down, a hard refusal that made her gag.

Rachel leaned into the wall and waited until she could see again.

The voices drifted closer, then passed, boots soft on stone.

As they went by, she caught a word.

"Another concurrent case."

The phrase landed and stuck.

She pushed away from the wall and kept moving, because the pressure did not give her a choice.

The corridor narrowed into a crawl. She ducked under a pipe and felt it vibrate, a low tremor running through the metal as if the whole system were waking up around her.

A drip hit the back of her neck. She flinched. The pressure twitched, annoyed.

Ahead, the passage opened into a taller shaft with a ladder bolted to the wall. Above it, faint light and voices. Below it, darkness and the smell of wet stone.

Rachel grabbed the ladder.

The pressure flared. Nausea hit. Her hands slipped.

She let go and stumbled back, breathing hard.

The ladder rattled anyway. A pipe above her burst with a sharp hiss, spraying hot water into the shaft. Someone yelped from above. The sound cut off, replaced by hurried footsteps.

Rachel backed away, heart racing. She had not touched the valve. The pressure had, in its blunt way, closed the exposure before it happened.

She turned and limped deeper into the dark, the only direction it would let her go.

# Chapter 14 — A Junction Lit for No One

The maintenance run ended in a junction that had been lit for no one.

Lines in the floor glowed faintly. Doors to either side sat half open, as if the corridor could not decide which route was safe.

Rachel stepped into the junction and felt the pressure tighten, alert and ready.

Someone was waiting.

Three people in dark coats stood at the far end, spaced out, hands visible, a strip of pale material stretched between two of them like a leash.

"Stop," one said, calm and flat.

Rachel froze.

The strip on the floor brightened. A shallow groove lit beneath her boots, hemming her in.

She looked left. The pressure shoved hard and she gagged, vision tunneling.

She looked right. The same. The pressure was not letting her near the doors.

The nearest person took one step forward. The strip between them hummed.

Rachel's chest burned. The pressure coiled, hot and tight, like a fist held back.

"Do not touch her," someone said from farther back. "If she spikes, drop it."

The strip hummed again.

Rachel took one step backward. The groove behind her lit.

She realized then that the only unlit path was forward, toward the strip.

She breathed in, then moved.

The pressure flared, not in refusal but in defense, and the stone under the strip warped with a low groan. The person holding it staggered as the floor dipped.

Rachel did not wait. She lunged into the gap, shoulder down, and the strip snapped as she passed, its pale material tearing with a sharp, dry sound.

"Now," someone shouted.

The corridor ahead opened into a cart lane, rails set into the floor, a slow-moving line of empty carts sliding by.

Rachel threw herself onto the nearest one. Her leg screamed. She bit down and held on as the cart pulled her forward.

Behind her, the floor lines flared. A shout broke. Something slammed into the stone where she had been.

The cart carried her into the next corridor, and the pressure settled heavy and focused, not toward safety but toward motion.

The cart line climbed.

Rachel clung to the rail as the corridor angled upward, the air warming, the sound of distant voices bleeding in from somewhere above. The pressure in her chest tightened with every rise, a steady refusal that made her teeth ache.

She could hear them now. People. Too many. A market hum, or a shift change, or something that meant open space.

The pressure slammed hard.

Not a voice. A single word pressed into her skull: Away.

Rachel cried out and tumbled off the cart as a side gate flashed open beside her. She hit the floor, rolled, and the gate snapped shut behind her with a heavy clang.

The cart line kept climbing. The voices faded.

Rachel lay on cold stone, gasping, and realized the pressure had not saved her from pain. It had saved her from being seen.

She pushed herself up and found a narrow passage, low and wet, the floor slick. Water pooled in the center, black and still.

On the wall, someone had scratched a line with something sharp. The marks were not letters she knew, but the meaning slid into her head anyway.

DO NOT LET THEM SEE YOU.

Below it, a second line.

THEY DO NOT STOP.

Rachel stared until her eyes burned.

The pressure tightened, urging her forward. She tore her gaze away and limped on, the words following her like a hand at her back.

# Chapter 15 — The Corridor Woke

The passage opened into a corridor that had been dark and was not anymore.

A thin strip of light ran along the ceiling, pale and steady, then another came on farther down, then another, like stepping stones calling her forward.

Rachel froze. The pressure tightened a beat later, sharp and sudden, as if it had noticed the lights at the same moment she did.

The hum in the walls deepened, uneven, like the building was pulling power from places it had meant to keep asleep.

She moved because the pressure would not let her stand still.

Each time she stepped, another light came on ahead. Each time she tried to slow, nausea tightened behind her eyes.

A click sounded inside the walls. Then another, deeper, farther away. The sound did not travel right. It bent around corners it should not have reached.

Rachel's chest burned. The pressure spread low and wide in her ribs, not flaring, not pulling back, just rearranging itself in a way that made her skin prickle.

She reached a door that was not a door a moment ago. It sat half open, cold air breathing out.

She took one step toward it.

The pressure slammed into her. She staggered back, breath tearing, and the door slid shut.

The lights ahead brightened again, urging her onward.

Rachel swore and kept moving.

Behind her, the corridor lights did not turn off.

They followed.

The corridor ended at a junction cut into older stone, the walls pocked with bolt holes and old seams that had been sealed and reopened too many times.

Rachel stopped and felt the pressure tighten, uncertain, as if the place did not have a clear rule for what she was supposed to do next.

A thin device slid from a slit in the wall.

It was no larger than her forearm, a pale rod with a faint blue line along its edge. It hovered, humming softly, and the line flickered as it turned toward her.

Rachel froze.

The pressure went still, the held breath absence that made her teeth ache. Something inside her listened.

The rod drifted closer.

She took one step back.

The rod paused. The line brightened.

Rachel felt heat rise under her skin, the pressure coiling hard, ready to flare. She forced herself still, jaw clenched, arms shaking.

The rod retracted a fraction. The hum changed, higher now, curious.

From somewhere beyond the wall, a voice carried, muffled and urgent. "It is on the fallback line."

"Keep it moving," another voice answered. "No contact."

She needed out. Daylight. Anything that was not this.

The pressure twitched at the word contact, a reflexive refusal.

The rod extended again.

Rachel broke.

She shoved herself sideways into a narrow service gap, shoulder scraping stone, and the pressure flared just enough to shove the rod back against the wall. It clattered, its light stuttering.

A tone chirped once. Not an alarm. A signal.

Rachel kept moving, slipping through the gap into a darker run that smelled of dust and old oil. Behind her, the hum shifted, and she heard the rod retreat, the slit sealing with a soft click.

The pressure did not ease. It pushed her on, away from the voices, away from the line of light that now traced the corridor she had left.

She limped into the dark, and the lights followed at a distance she could feel.

# Chapter 16 — The Light Followed Her In

The dark run opened into a larger chamber, and the light followed her in.

A thin band slid under the door behind her, then through the seams in the walls, pale and wrong against the stone. The pressure tightened in her chest, sharp and sudden, like it had reacted before she did.

Rachel pressed her back to the wall and listened.

Clicks answered from the corridor. Not footsteps. Not voices. Something mechanical, patient.

The door she had passed through unsealed.

The pressure coiled.

A unit rolled in on a low track, no taller than her knee, a ring of metal with a hollow center and a line of light that swept the room in a slow circle. It moved with the same calm certainty as the facility itself.

Rachel held her breath.

The light swept past her and paused. The ring hummed, deeper now, as if it had found something that matched.

The pressure flared, hot and insistent, and Rachel felt the choice tighten around her ribs: stay hidden and let it act, or move and be seen.

She pushed off the wall.

The ring pivoted toward her.

The light locked on.
# Chapter 17 — Light Before Sound

The light reached her boots before the sound reached her ears.

A narrow band slid across the floor, pale and steady, cutting the dark into measured sections. It stopped when it touched the toe of her left boot and stayed there, unwavering, as if waiting for something to line up.

Rachel froze.

Pressure gathered under her ribs. Not pain. Not yet. Just enough weight to warn her that stillness was the wrong answer.
Her body recognized the weight before she did.

She shifted her weight.

The light brightened a fraction.
She tried it again, smaller. The light answered again.

Something hummed behind it. Low and even, the sound of a system running exactly as intended.

Rachel stepped back. The stone beneath her heel warmed, heat blooming too fast to be natural. The pressure tightened, nausea rising sharp and fast, and she lurched sideways instead, catching herself on the wall as the light slid forward and settled where she had been standing.

The unit rolled into view.

It stayed low to the ground, a wide ring moving without visible joints. The light came from inside it, sweeping outward in slow arcs that never crossed the walls, never rushed ahead.
She knew its sweep pattern, the pause at her left boot. The knowledge arrived ahead of memory.

It followed at a fixed distance.

Rachel limped down the corridor, favoring her bad leg, every step negotiated between pain and the pressure urging her onward. The walls narrowed ahead, seams faintly glowing as systems woke and adjusted.

The unit followed.

Not closer.

Close enough.

The pressure shoved her right at the junction before she registered the turn. She tried to hold the shove, and for half a step it eased. Then it surged again. She scraped her shoulder on stone and bit back a sound as nausea hit hard enough to steal her breath. Black crept at the edges of her vision. She gagged and kept moving.

Behind her, the hum deepened by a note.

The corridor dipped into a service run lined with pipes. Water dripped steadily into a channel cut down the center of the floor. Rust flaked under her fingers as she crossed, boots slipping on damp stone.

The pressure flared as her foot slid. She went down on one knee, the impact jolting pain up her leg, and braced herself against the wall.

The unit stopped at the threshold.

Its light swept the space again, slower now, as if taking inventory.

Rachel dragged herself upright and limped toward the far end of the run. Her leg burned, then went strangely numb, sensation thinning until it felt distant and hollow. The pressure settled, dense and focused, bracing her instead of driving her.

Overhead, a pipe rattled as she passed. Another shuddered, vibrating in a way that made her teeth ache. The pressure twisted, sharp and insistent, and the pipe burst with a crack, spraying hot water into the channel.

Steam filled the run.

The unit rolled forward.

Its light cut through the steam and dimmed, just slightly, before correcting.

Rachel ran.

Not fast. Not clean. Her body moved because it was already moving, because stopping felt worse than falling. The corridor bent twice and sloped downward, the floor slick beneath her boots.

The pressure surged ahead of her, yanking her left at the next split before she could think about it. She slammed a hand into the wall to stay upright, palm skidding over stone.

The surface puckered under her touch.

A crack raced outward and vanished around the bend.

She pulled her hand back and kept going.

The hum behind her changed pitch.

She burst into a wider passage and skidded to a halt.

A man stood near a stack of crates, hands lifting slowly as he saw her. A dull white knot marked his vest, the same one she had seen painted on doors. Not a rank. A warning. He touched it once, a habit more prayer than signal. His gaze flicked to her leg, to the faint pulse beneath her skin, and his throat worked.

“Don’t,” he said.

Rachel opened her mouth.

The pressure slammed down and stole the word.

The hum grew louder.

The man’s eyes slid past her shoulder. Color drained from his face.

“Go,” he said, louder now. “Go now.”

Rachel tried to move past him.

The pressure flared sideways.

The floor between them dipped, just enough to knock him off balance. He stumbled into the crates and went down hard, a sharp cry torn from his throat.

“No,” Rachel gasped. “I didn’t—”

The unit entered the passage.

The light locked onto the fallen man.

The pressure recoiled, tight and offended, then braced hard against Rachel’s ribs.

She turned and ran.

The corridor narrowed into a crawl. She ducked and scraped and dragged herself through, the hum echoing too loud behind her, too close. A door slid open as she hit it shoulder-first, and she tumbled through.

The door slammed shut with a clang that rattled the walls.

The hum cut off.

Rachel lay on the floor, chest heaving, forehead pressed to cold stone. Feeling rushed back into her leg in a hot, punishing wave. She waited for the pressure to move her.

It didn’t.

It held.

She pushed herself up and looked around.

The room was small and square. A single light strip hummed overhead. A bench sat against one wall. Near the ceiling, a narrow grate spilled in cooler air that smelled of oil and dust.

Lena crouched beside the bench.

She looked thinner. One arm was wrapped in a stained bandage. Her eyes flicked to the door, then back to Rachel, sharp and quick.

“Don’t stand there,” Lena said. “It’ll find the heat.”

Rachel stared. Her mouth opened, closed.

“You’re alive,” she said.

Lena snorted softly. “I noticed.”

The pressure twitched under Rachel’s ribs.

“How did you—”

“Later.” Lena stood and grabbed Rachel’s arm, fingers digging in hard enough to hurt. “Move.”

She dragged Rachel toward a low panel set into the wall. It slid aside at her touch, revealing a dark slot barely wide enough for one person.

“Go.”

Rachel hesitated.

The pressure leaned forward.

She dropped to her knees and crawled, scraping shoulder and hip, biting back a sound as pain flared. Lena followed and slammed the panel shut behind them.

They crawled in the dark. The space stank of old metal and damp stone. Rachel’s breath rasped loud in her ears. The pressure stayed heavy and still, like it was listening.

The slot opened into a maintenance shaft. Lena rolled out and hauled Rachel after her, bracing her weight without comment.

They moved.

Down ladders. Through side runs. Past places Rachel remembered being shoved away from before. Lena didn’t slow. She moved like someone who had memorized where things failed.

“Why were you there?” Rachel asked between breaths.

“Because they put me where things break.”

Behind them, the hum started again.

Farther away.

Rachel stumbled. Lena swore and pulled her upright.

“That thing’s tighter now,” Lena said.

“It locked on,” Rachel said.

Lena’s mouth flattened. “I figured.”

They reached a junction marked with three scratched symbols. Lena paused, head tilted, listening.

The pressure twitched toward the left passage.

Lena turned right.

The pressure flared hot and sudden, nausea tearing up Rachel’s throat. She gagged and grabbed the wall, vision swimming.

“Stop,” Rachel gasped.

Lena hesitated.

The hum deepened behind them.

“Trust me,” Lena said.

The pressure surged again, harder this time. Rachel cried out as her knees buckled.

Lena cursed and dragged her into the right-hand passage anyway.

The nausea eased, settling into a dull, workable throb. Rachel could see again. She could keep her feet.

They ran.

The corridor opened into a low passage lined with old doors. Some stood open. Some were sealed. Pale light leaked from beneath a few of them, thin and wrong.

“We don’t take doors,” Lena said, slowing. “We take gaps.”

She kicked a loose panel. It fell inward, revealing another narrow run.

They slipped through as the hum rounded the corner behind them. The panel slid back into place.

Rachel slumped against the wall, shaking.

“Why help me?” she said. “They took you because of me.”

Lena watched the seam in the panel, listening. When she spoke, her voice stayed low.

“They take people all the time,” she said. “You just make it faster.”

The pressure shifted, uneasy.

“You’re not from here,” Rachel said.

Lena glanced at her. “Neither are you. Not after a circle spits you out.”

“Where are you from?”

Lena snorted. “Someplace that doesn’t matter anymore.”

The hum passed on the other side of the wall, light bleeding briefly through the seam before fading.

Silence followed.

“There are others,” Lena said. “Routes. People who move cargo.”

“Cargo,” Rachel said.

“That’s us.”

The pressure tightened, not in warning but in agreement.

“There’s a place,” Lena continued. “Past the routes. Past the lines. Where they stop looking.”

Rachel swallowed. “Free.”

Lena’s mouth twitched. “Alive,” she said. “Sometimes that’s close enough.”

The pressure leaned forward, not toward the wall, but toward the dark ahead.

Lena stood. “We move before it recalibrates.”

Rachel pushed herself up. Her leg protested, then held.

They slipped deeper into the dark as the stone behind them began, quietly, to warm.

# Chapter 18 — A Mistake in the Wall

The gap wasn’t a tunnel so much as a mistake the wall hadn’t corrected yet.

Stone bowed inward, then out again, its surface uneven in a way Rachel couldn’t keep her eyes on for long. If she stared, her vision slipped; if she didn’t, the sense of movement stayed anyway, like the place was remembering how it should have been shaped.

Lena went first, not pausing, not checking.

Rachel followed and felt the pressure crowd her chest, heavy and unhelpful, not guiding her so much as waiting to see what she’d do wrong. The air on the other side smelled scraped clean—dry metal, old heat, something burned out and never replaced.

Crates filled the room, stacked with too much care for a place this temporary. Chalk lines crossed the floor in shapes that almost closed, their edges blurred where boots had scuffed them away. Rachel stepped over one without thinking and felt the pressure tighten, sharp and irritated, like she’d kicked a live wire.

Someone shifted in the dark.

A woman stepped forward, short and thick through the shoulders, gloves darkened at the fingertips. She raised a handheld scanner, its casing cracked, its buzz uneven and insect-thin.

She looked at Lena.

Then at Rachel.

The scanner screamed.

“Fuck,” the woman said, already moving back.

Lena snapped, “Cut it.”

The woman didn’t argue. She smashed the scanner against the wall until it went silent, the sound echoing too long, too clean.

“You didn’t say it was like this,” she said, eyes still on Rachel. “You said injured. You said manageable.”

Rachel felt her leg pulse under her skin and hated that her body had learned to announce itself.

“I said she moves,” Lena replied. “That’s all.”

The woman let out a short laugh that held no humor. “She doesn’t move. She ruins.”

The floor shifted under Rachel’s feet, not enough to throw her, just enough to make her stomach flip. One of the chalk lines cracked and burned away to bare stone.

The hum wasn’t there yet, but the room felt thinner, like it had been marked.

Lights dimmed, brightened, settled wrong.

“We won’t stay,” Rachel said, her voice louder than she meant it to be.

“You won’t,” the woman said. “I won’t either if you leave right now.”

Lena grabbed Rachel’s arm and pulled her toward the far wall, boots scraping as the floor pitched again, a fraction steeper this time.

“No,” the woman said. “You’re burning my node.”

The pressure swelled without direction, dense and clumsy. A crate slid an inch across the floor and bumped another with a hollow sound.

“Stop,” Lena said, not looking at Rachel.

Rachel tried to still herself and failed. The pressure didn’t care about intent.

“You know what happens next,” the woman said. “You know how fast they trace a summoning spill.”

“I know,” Lena said, too quickly.

The hum arrived like the start of a migraine, low and present, close enough to feel in Rachel’s teeth.

The woman moved fast, ripping open a crate and shoving a bundle into Lena’s hands. “Route three is dead. Don’t touch doors. If the walls start sweating, you run.”

“We always run,” Lena said.

The woman’s gaze slid back to Rachel. “Not fast enough.”

The crate beside Rachel split with a sharp crack, metal spilling across the floor.

The woman bolted.

Lights snapped off as she ran, plunging the room into uneven emergency glow. Somewhere deeper, a door sealed hard enough to shake dust loose from the ceiling.

Lena dragged Rachel into the dark and didn’t slow when Rachel stumbled.

They ran through a passage that narrowed too quickly, Rachel clipping her shoulder hard enough to tear skin. The walls pulsed faintly as they passed, heat bleeding through stone like a fever coming on.

The pressure crowded her forward, dragging her through turns she hadn’t seen yet, leaving her breathless and angry and unable to tell which part of her was driving.

The hum thickened.

Rachel’s leg burned, then didn’t. The pain didn’t leave; it slid somewhere duller, less urgent, and she hated the quiet more than the ache.

They burst into another chamber.

Smaller. Cleaner. A man waited there with one hand on a lever set into the wall, eyes already glassy like he’d been listening too long.

He looked at Lena, then at Rachel, and shook his head once.

“No.”

Lena didn’t slow. “Open it.”

“You’re too loud,” he said, voice thin.

The pressure spread, sloppy and wide, brushing the room. The lever rattled in its housing as the wall behind him bowed inward, stone groaning.

“I can’t,” he said. “If I do, they follow me.”

“They follow you anyway,” Lena said. “She’s here.”

Rachel took a step forward without meaning to, and the pressure surged with her, uncontrolled. A seam in the ceiling lit up.

The man’s mouth worked. He swallowed.

“Vent,” he said. “Don’t touch the floor plates.”

He yanked the lever.

A panel shrieked open and heat rolled out, dry and biting. Lena shoved Rachel toward it without slowing.

Rachel dropped to her hands and knees and crawled, metal scraping skin, the panel slamming shut behind them with a vibration she felt in her bones.

The hum filled the chamber outside.

Rachel felt it through the vent, not sound so much as resonance, tapping at the inside of her skull. The pressure coiled, disorganized and angry, as they crawled until the vent gave way and Rachel spilled into a narrow shaft lined with old insulation.

She landed wrong and waited for her leg to fail.

It didn’t.

Her hands stopped shaking faster than they should have. Her breathing evened before she was ready for it.

Lena watched her closely. “It’s faster.”

Rachel nodded. “It’s spreading.”

Lena didn’t argue.

They moved again, following scratches carved into the walls by different hands, different tools, some old, some fresh, all pointing deeper.

“Why did she run?” Rachel asked.

Lena hesitated just long enough to matter. “Because you don’t pass clean. No one does after you.”

The hum echoed above them, closer now.

Rachel stopped. “You knew.”

“I know how long places last,” Lena said, meeting her eyes.

The pressure crowded her chest, sharp and demanding.

“Where does this go?” Rachel asked.

“Another handoff,” Lena said. “If they’ll take us.”

“And if they won’t?”

Lena looked away. “Then we keep breaking things.”

Ahead, the wall shimmered faintly. Rachel’s palm tingled before she touched it, like the stone was already aware of her.

The pressure surged forward.

Not waiting.

Not asking.

# Chapter 19 — Not a Place That Helps

The next place didn’t look like a place that helped.

It looked like a throat.

The passage narrowed unevenly, walls sweating faintly where the stone met old metal braces. The air tasted sour, like insulation that had burned once and never been replaced. Someone had scraped symbols into the wall with a tool that skipped, lines gouged too deep and then abandoned.

Lena slowed.

Not because she wanted to.

Because the pressure did something strange.

It didn’t surge. It didn’t crowd. It slid sideways, like it had noticed something Rachel hadn’t yet.

Rachel’s foot caught on a raised seam and she swore, the sound bouncing wrong in the narrow space. Her leg held, but she felt the delay now—the fraction of time between impact and response stretching, rubbery and unpleasant.

“That’s bad,” Lena muttered.

“What?” Rachel asked.

Lena didn’t answer.

They reached the end of the passage and stepped into a room that wasn’t finished deciding what it was. Half concrete, half framed, wiring exposed like veins. A single lamp hung from a cord, swaying gently even though nothing had touched it.

A man sat at a folding table with his hands flat on the surface.

He didn’t stand when he saw them.

He looked at Lena first, then at Rachel, and something in his face closed.

“No,” he said.

Lena stopped short. “We just need—”

“No,” he said again, firmer this time. “You can’t be here.”

Rachel felt the pressure press outward, unfocused, brushing the edges of the room. The lamp flickered. The cord creaked.

“We won’t stay,” Rachel said. The words came out fast, overlapping themselves. “We just need to pass through.”

The man’s gaze stayed on her, steady and unhappy. “That’s what the last one said. The last off-worlder.”

Lena’s jaw tightened. “She’s injured.”

“Rooms don’t stay the same after you,” he replied.

The word hit wrong.

Rachel felt it in her chest, not fear exactly, but a kind of recognition, like when someone names a problem you’ve been pretending is temporary.

The pressure shifted again, jittery now, tapping at the room in uneven pulses. A screw worked loose from the ceiling brace and dropped, clinking on the concrete.

The man flinched.

“I won’t open it,” he said. “I won’t mark another route.”

Lena stepped forward. “If you don’t, they’ll catch her in the open.”

“If I do, they’ll catch all of us,” he said. “You know how this works.”

Rachel glanced around, trying to anchor on something solid. The table wobbled slightly under the man’s hands. One leg was shorter than the others. She fixated on that, on the uneven scrape it made when he shifted his weight.

The pressure coiled tight, crowded and irritated.

“I can be careful,” Rachel said, and hated how small it sounded.

The man shook his head. “You can’t. That’s the point.”

The lamp flickered again, brighter this time. The walls gave off a faint, skin-prickling warmth.

Lena swore under her breath. “We’re out of time.”

The hum arrived without ceremony.

No buildup. No warning.

Just a low vibration threading through the room, setting Rachel’s teeth on edge and making the lamp swing wider, cord twisting as if it were trying to unwind itself.

The man stood up too fast, chair scraping loudly across the floor. “You brought it here?”

Rachel felt the pressure snap inward, hard enough to steal her breath. Not a push. A clamp.

Her vision tunneled. The room tilted.

She staggered and caught herself on the table. The surface bowed under her palm, wood fibers creaking as if they were softening.

“I didn’t—” she started.

The pressure surged before she finished the thought.

Not outward.

Down.

The concrete floor cracked with a sharp, echoing pop. A fissure raced from beneath Rachel’s hand to the far wall, splitting around the man’s feet.

He shouted and stumbled back, hitting the wall hard enough to rattle the exposed wiring. Sparks spat and died.

Lena grabbed Rachel around the ribs and hauled her back. “Stop fighting it!”

“I’m not!” Rachel gasped.

The hum deepened.

Not louder. Denser. Like something had decided this room was no longer optional.

The man backed toward a metal door set into the far wall, fumbling for the latch. “Get out,” he said, voice breaking. “Get out now.”

The pressure twisted, confused and angry, then did something new.

It moved Rachel.

Not a shove. Not a pull.

Her weight shifted without her choosing it, balance snapping forward as if her body had skipped a step. She lurched, collided with Lena, and both of them went down hard.

The door behind the man buckled inward.

Metal screamed.

The latch tore free and skittered across the floor.

The man stared at it, then at Rachel, horror finally overtaking caution. “What are you?”

Rachel didn’t have an answer.

The pressure surged again, wider this time, sloppier, brushing everything at once. The lamp tore free from the ceiling and smashed against the wall, glass exploding outward in a bright, sharp spray.

The hum cut off.

For half a second, everything went wrong-quiet.

Then Lena was dragging Rachel up by the collar. “Move. Now.”

They ran through the only opening left, not a door so much as a split where the wall had failed. Rachel’s foot slipped on loose concrete and she nearly went down again, hands scraping raw as she caught herself.

Behind them, the room collapsed inward with a heavy, final sound.

They didn’t stop running.

They didn’t talk.

Rachel’s heart hammered too fast, breath tearing in and out of her chest. Her leg held. Her hands steadied faster than they should have. The pressure stayed close, crowded and sharp, like it was braced for another mistake.

They ducked into a narrow side run and Lena slammed a panel shut behind them. The hum didn’t follow.

Rachel slid down the wall, shaking.

“That wasn’t supposed to happen,” she said.

Lena crouched in front of her, face pale and furious. “No,” she said. “That was.”

Rachel looked up. “You said there were routes.”

“There are,” Lena said. “And then there are limits.”

The pressure pressed against Rachel’s ribs, restless, offended.

“What did you do?” Rachel whispered.

Lena didn’t answer right away. When she did, her voice was tight. “I brought you somewhere that could say no.”

Rachel swallowed, throat burning. “And?”

Lena stood. “And it couldn’t.”

The walls around them creaked softly, warming as if something deeper had shifted its attention.

Lena wiped her hands on her pants and looked down the passage ahead. “We don’t get many of those.”

Rachel pushed herself up, legs unsteady but working. Her body felt wrong—lighter somehow, quicker to respond, like the space between thought and motion had thinned.

The pressure leaned forward.

Already moving.

# Chapter 20 — Losing the Floor

They didn’t slow down so much as lose the floor.

The passage narrowed, dipped, and then ended where part of it had fallen away. Rachel pitched forward, caught herself on her hands, and slid the rest of the way down on her knees, breath knocked loose from her chest.

Lena hit the wall beside her and swore.

Nothing followed.

That sat wrong in Rachel’s gut.

She stayed where she was, palms stinging, waiting for the hum, for the vibration in her teeth, for the pressure to shove her upright again. Instead it crowded her chest and stayed there, tight and close, like it had pressed its ear to the stone.

Water dripped nearby. The air smelled cracked open.

“Don’t move,” Lena said.

Rachel didn’t.

The pressure shifted.

Not forward.

Sideways.

Rachel’s balance went strange, just a fraction off, and her eyes slid toward the wall where the collapse had peeled back layers that didn’t belong in open air. Stone had torn away, leaving metal exposed, twisted inward like it had been pulled instead of struck.

A door sat there.

Bent. Half-seated in a frame that no longer matched it.

Lena saw it and stopped breathing for a second.

“That’s not sealed,” she said.

Rachel opened her mouth. Nothing came out.

The door flexed.

Not opening.

Giving.

The pressure snapped.

Rachel’s weight shifted hard to the left. Her knees skidded on damp stone and her shoulder slammed into the wall.

The door tore free.

Metal shrieked as it folded inward, frame ripping loose in a way that sounded too easy.

Something inside screamed.

High. Short.

It stopped.

Rachel tasted blood and swallowed hard.

Lena shouted her name.

The pressure surged again, wider now, brushing past Rachel and into the space she hadn’t meant to uncover.

Silence dropped heavy.

Rachel pushed herself upright. Her legs answered immediately. She was standing before she remembered standing.

“I—” she said.

Nothing followed it.

Lena grabbed her shoulders and shook her once. “What did you just do?”

Rachel’s heart hammered, but her hands were still. Her breathing had already evened out.

Lena turned toward the room.

Light hummed softly inside. Clean panels. The kind of room you woke up in when the summon held and nobody panicked. A body against the far wall, limbs bent where they shouldn’t bend. Blood spread slowly, following the grooves cut into the floor.

Rachel stared at the blood, not the person.

“I didn’t touch them,” she said.

Lena didn’t turn. “You didn’t have to.”

The pressure shifted again, tight and restless.

“That room wasn’t empty,” Lena said. “It wasn’t supposed to be.”

Rachel took a step forward before she noticed she’d moved. The pressure stayed close to her ribs.

“No,” Lena said. “Stay.”

Rachel stopped.

The room smelled clean under the blood.

Prepared.

“I didn’t—” Rachel tried again.

Lena cut her off. “Don’t.”

They stood there too long.

The hum came back, thin and distant, threading through the walls like a sound remembered instead of heard.

Lena grabbed Rachel’s wrist and pulled. “We’re gone.”

They ran.

The passage bent in on itself. Rachel stumbled, caught herself, and kept moving, feet landing where she expected them to be without checking first.

“Did it—” she started.

Lena didn’t answer.

They burst into a maintenance bay cluttered with stripped braces and broken equipment. Heat sat heavy in the air. Lena shoved a rack aside and tore open a crawlspace behind it.

“Inside.”

Rachel hesitated.

The pressure crowded her chest.

She crawled.

The space forced her onto her side, metal pressing into her ribs, vibrating faintly. Lena slammed the panel shut behind them and wedged it with something heavy.

They lay there.

Rachel’s heartbeat slowed.

Too quickly.

Her breathing evened out. Her muscles loosened.

She waited for the shaking.

It didn’t come.

“I should—” she whispered.

Lena’s voice came thin through the dark. “Don’t.”

The hum passed overhead and kept going.

The pressure stayed close, tight against Rachel’s ribs, not pushing, not leaving.

“When did it start doing that?” Rachel asked.

Lena didn’t answer.

Somewhere deeper in the structure, metal shifted with a soft, final sound.

Rachel stared into the dark, listening to her own breathing, waiting for something to catch up to her that didn’t.

Nothing did.

# Chapter 21 — Slowed Steps

Rachel slowed her steps until the effort made her calves burn.

The passage sloped downward, walls damp where old braces showed through the stone, the floor uneven in a way that punished hesitation. Lena moved ahead of her, shoulders tight, counting under her breath without realizing she was doing it.

Rachel shortened her stride again, forcing her weight to settle where her foot landed instead of pitching forward into the next step. It worked badly. Her knee locked for half a beat, then released too late, sending her hand into the wall as she caught herself.

The stone was warm.

“That’s not helping,” Lena said, not turning around.

“I’m not running,” Rachel said, breath already out of sync with the pace she’d chosen.

The pressure crowded her chest without direction, dense and present, the way air feels before a storm breaks.

They moved on.

Rachel kept counting, letting the numbers stack up in her head, focusing on the scrape of her boots and the ache blooming behind her knee. The passage bent, shallow at first, then sharper, and the shift in angle arrived early, her foot already committed before her eyes caught up.

She stumbled and clipped the corner with her shoulder. Something inside the wall cracked low and ugly.

Lena swore and grabbed her arm. “You can’t change pace like that.”

“I didn’t,” Rachel said. “I went slower.”

“That’s the problem.”

The corridor opened into a wider chamber where the ceiling sagged between failed braces and rubble lay scattered in uneven drifts. Old equipment lay half-buried, metal edges catching faint light.

Rachel stopped moving forward and didn’t move backward either. She stayed exactly where she was and focused on the space between breaths, the way her lungs pulled air without instruction.

Her heart slowed.

The room didn’t.

Her balance slid downward, knees buckling as if the floor had dropped a fraction she couldn’t see. She hit hard, pain arriving late and wrong, muted like it had taken a detour.

Rachel pushed herself upright before she remembered falling. Her hands were steady. Her breath had already evened out.

Lena stared at her. “You didn’t brace.”

“I didn’t have time.”

The pressure stayed close, tight and unsettled, like something crowding into a space it didn’t quite fit.

They crossed the chamber faster than Rachel meant to, her steps stretching despite the effort she put into shortening them. She watched her hands instead of the floor, the way they lifted and reached a half-second before she finished deciding to move.

At the far end, a narrow stairwell dropped away into shadow.

Rachel slowed again, not stopping, just easing back against the forward pull of motion. Her foot slid farther than she meant it to, heel skidding on loose grit.

She yanked it back.

Her weight followed anyway.

Rachel lurched toward the stairwell and caught the rail with both hands, metal biting into her palms as her boots scraped uselessly for purchase.

Lena lunged and wrapped an arm around her waist. “Let go!”

“No,” Rachel said, the word tearing out of her.

The rail tore free.

Metal screamed as it ripped from the wall, and the world tipped sideways as Rachel and Lena went down together, tumbling the first few steps before slamming into the landing below.

They lay there, stunned, breath ragged.

Rachel waited for the crash to catch up to her.

It didn’t.

Her leg answered when she tested it. Her hands stopped shaking almost immediately. Her heart slowed faster than it should have.

Lena pushed herself up, face pale and furious. “You see it now?”

Rachel nodded.

“It’s not speed,” she said, voice flat. “It’s timing.”

The pressure pressed close without correcting her.

They didn’t argue after that.

They moved.

Rachel stopped trying to adjust her pace. Instead, she watched the lag between thought and motion, the way the decision arrived after the movement had already begun.

Every attempt to interfere bent the space around her just enough to make things worse.

By the time they reached the next run, Rachel was breathing evenly, sweat cooling on her skin, fear settling somewhere distant and manageable.

That scared her more than the fall.

They slipped into the dark, stone warming behind them, the pressure already leaning forward, lining her up with whatever came next.

# Chapter 22 — Not Found

They didn’t find the place.

The place took them.

The corridor looked like all the others until the floor changed under Rachel’s boots—not a step or a slope, just stone worn smoother in a strip wide enough to notice too late. Lena slowed without deciding to. Rachel didn’t and nearly ran into her, breath catching hard as she checked herself.

“There,” Lena said, and it wasn’t relief.

Rachel looked for a door and didn’t see one. Just a seam in the wall that didn’t line up with anything around it, stone patched wrong, like a repair that had been rushed and then forgotten. A chalk line ran down the seam and stopped at ankle height, the mark broken off mid-stroke.

Lena lifted her hand and let it hover.

The pressure crowded Rachel’s ribs, tight and restless, then slid away from the seam as if it had brushed something unpleasant.

Lena tapped the stone once.

Nothing.

She tapped again, harder this time, knuckles whitening.

A small click answered from inside the wall, neat and final, like the end of a mechanism. The seam folded inward with stubborn reluctance. Warm air spilled out—soap, old coffee, something chemical underneath that burned faintly at the back of Rachel’s throat.

Lena went in.

Rachel followed and felt the pressure recoil, then tighten again a half-second later, undecided.

The seam closed behind them with a quiet drag of stone that made Rachel flinch anyway.

The room was low-ceilinged and badly lit, bare bulbs hanging too close together, throwing overlapping shadows that never quite settled. Tables lined the walls. Crates stacked in corners. A kettle hissed softly on a hot plate, steam curling up and vanishing before it reached the ceiling. Someone had tried very hard to make it ordinary.
A cord with three tight knots hung above the seam, wax-stiff, the kind people touched before crossing.

Three people looked up.

A gray-haired man sat at a table with both hands around a mug he wasn’t drinking, shoulders hunched as if he were cold.

A young woman stood near the far wall with a toolbox at her feet, sleeves rolled up, eyes too bright, like she’d been awake longer than she should have been.

An older woman stood closest to the seam, small and wiry, already turning her body so she wasn’t directly in line with the door. She didn’t block it. She listened to it, palm lifted toward the stone without touching. Under the bare bulbs, her skin held a faint blue cast, not sick, just wrong.

Her gaze moved from Lena to Rachel and stayed there.

“Route?” she asked.

Lena opened her mouth. Closed it again.

“Name,” the woman said.

“Lena.”

“And you.”

Rachel swallowed. The pressure crowded her chest.

“Rachel.”

The woman nodded once, then pointed with two fingers. “Sit.”

The chair was near the wall, away from the table, away from the seam.

Rachel moved toward it carefully. One of the bulbs flickered, steadied, then flickered again.

She sat. The chair scraped. She shifted it a fraction to stop the sound.

The scrape got louder, sharper, like it was arguing.

Rachel froze.

“Don’t,” the woman said, and this time it wasn’t gentle.

Rachel lifted her hands slowly, palms open, heat prickling across her skin. “I’m not—”

“You’re not the one who decides when things stop,” the woman said, eyes never leaving Rachel’s chest.

The gray-haired man finally drank from his mug. His hand shook. He kept the mug close to his mouth to hide it.

Lena leaned against the wall near the seam, breathing hard, eyes tracking the ceiling braces, the bulbs, the cracks between them.

“What’s on you?” the woman asked.

Rachel blinked. “On me?”

The woman’s gaze didn’t move. “That.”

“I don’t know,” Rachel said.

The woman nodded, like that answer had weight. “Good.”

She turned her head. “Miri.”

The young woman dropped to her knees by the wall and pulled a panel free. Inside, wires were bundled too neatly, taped down in strips. Her hands shook as she worked. She didn’t look at Rachel.

“What is this place?” Rachel asked, her voice sounding too loud in the low room.

“A stop,” the woman said. “Sit still.”

Miri’s screwdriver slipped. Sparks jumped. One bulb dimmed. Another flickered.

Miri hissed and jerked her hand back, then forced it in again, jaw clenched.

“It’s not the wire,” the gray-haired man said without looking up.

The woman watched Rachel. “When you crossed the seam, it pulled.”

Rachel’s skin prickled. “Pulled what?”

The woman didn’t answer.

Silence settled in layers. Not calm. Listening.

The kettle clicked off.

The pressure crowded Rachel’s ribs, restless, like it wanted to surge and couldn’t find room.

The woman stepped back a pace. Then another, careful not to turn her back.

“We don’t touch you,” she said. “We don’t stand close. We don’t close you into smaller spaces than we have to.”

She said it like she’d said it before. Like she’d watched a room decide it didn’t like a person and fix the problem in the quickest way available.

Lena let out a short breath that might have been a laugh. “Safehouse.”

The word sat badly in the room.

“Drink,” the woman said, pointing to a bottle on the table. “Slow.”

Rachel leaned forward in pieces. The pressure pressed close, impatient. Her fingers closed around the bottle.

The table shuddered.

The gray-haired man hissed as his mug tipped, coffee spilling across the surface and dripping onto the floor. He yanked his hands back and stared at the mess like it had accused him of something.

Rachel unscrewed the cap and drank. The water tasted faintly of plastic and something medicinal.

Her heartbeat slowed too fast.

The room didn’t.

Miri shoved the panel closed and sat back on her heels, breathing hard, sweat beading along her hairline.

The woman pressed her palm to the seam again and stayed there longer this time, eyes closed.

“Is it close?” the man asked.

The woman didn’t answer at first. Then, quietly, “It’s here.”

Rachel’s stomach dropped.

“But not inside,” the woman added, and that wasn’t comfort. It was accounting.

The bulbs flickered in a wave down the line.

Miri made a sound that might have been a laugh and might have been a sob. “You brought it right to my hands.”

Lena turned toward Rachel. “We can’t stay.”

“You can’t leave yet,” the woman said.

Lena’s jaw tightened. “We don’t have a choice.”

The woman looked at Rachel. “You do.”

Rachel tightened her grip on the bottle. The plastic creaked and sprang back.

The pressure crowded her ribs and stayed there.

Not moving her.

Not leaving.

Just waiting.

# Chapter 23 — Not a Procedure

They didn’t call it a procedure.

They moved like people who didn’t want words sticking to anything.

Procedure was what you called it when you were calm enough to pretend it wasn’t panic. Nobody in this room looked that calm.

The older woman shifted the table first, dragging it a few inches farther from Rachel’s chair without asking. The legs shrieked against the floor. Rachel flinched and stopped herself from adjusting the chair again.

Miri stood up too fast and knocked her knee against the toolbox. She swore under her breath, pressed her palm hard against the bruise, and didn’t look at Rachel when she crossed the room.

“Don’t,” the older woman said, not raising her voice.

Miri froze.

The pressure crowded Rachel’s ribs, tight and irritated, as if it had leaned forward and found something in the way.

“We’re not closing anything,” the older woman said, more to the room than to Miri. “We’re widening.”

The gray-haired man set his mug down and pushed it away from himself. A dark ring marked the table where it had been.

Rachel watched the ring instead of their faces.

“What are you doing?” she asked.

No one answered right away.

Miri pulled a coil of chalk line from the toolbox and snapped it once, the sound sharp in the low room. She moved carefully, laying the line out in a loose arc that didn’t touch the walls, didn’t touch Rachel, didn’t close back on itself.

The pressure shifted, annoyed.

Rachel felt it lean toward the chalk, then pull back, like a hand testing heat.

“Grounding,” Lena said, not sounding convinced.

The older woman shook her head. “Deflection.”

Miri’s hands shook as she worked. The chalk left faint marks on the floor, uneven where her grip slipped. She wiped her palm on her pants and tried again.

Rachel’s chair creaked.

She didn’t move.

The pressure pressed closer, dense and watchful.

The gray-haired man stood and took two steps toward the seam, then stopped and leaned his shoulder against the wall instead. He closed his eyes.

Miri finished the arc and stepped back. “That’s as wide as I can make it.”

The older woman nodded. “Good. Now don’t cross it.”

Miri swallowed.

The older woman looked at Rachel. “This isn’t for you.”

Rachel opened her mouth. Closed it.

The pressure crowded her chest, then slid sideways, skimming the chalk line. One of the bulbs dimmed. Another flickered in response, late.

Miri hissed and clutched her wrist.

“What happened?” Lena asked.

Miri shook her head. “Nothing. It’s—” She stopped. Flexed her fingers. “Pins and needles.”

The pressure coiled tighter.

Rachel felt a pull—not on her body, but on her attention—dragging her focus toward the chalk arc, the unevenness in it, the place where the line thinned and nearly broke.

“Don’t look at it,” the older woman said.

Rachel looked away.

The pressure surged anyway.

The chalk line blurred.

Not smudged.

Shifted.

Miri gasped and staggered back, hand flying to her arm. “It’s—” She sucked in a breath. “It’s pulling.”

The older woman stepped back again, more distance now. “Step out.”

“I can’t,” Miri said. Her feet slid a fraction across the floor, soles whispering. “It’s like the floor’s—”

The chalk snapped.

The sound was small.

The effect wasn’t.

The arc collapsed inward, lines skidding across the floor toward the gap, chalk dust lifting into the air in a pale cloud.

The pressure lunged.

Rachel felt it move before she did. Her chair rocked back, then settled, the motion damped and wrong, like something had grabbed it mid-fall.

Miri screamed.

Not loud.

Breathless.

She dropped to her knees, clutching her arm as if something were sliding under the skin. The bulbs flickered wildly, then steadied.

The gray-haired man lurched forward and stopped short, fists clenched at his sides. “Miri.”

“Don’t touch her,” the older woman snapped.

Miri curled in on herself, breath coming fast and shallow. “It’s not pain,” she gasped. “It’s—wrong. It’s skipping.”

Rachel stared at Miri’s arm.

The skin there wasn’t torn.

It was… off. The outline of the muscle shifted a fraction late when Miri moved, like a reflection lagging behind.

Rachel’s stomach dropped.

“I didn’t—” she started.

The older woman cut her off. “No.”

The pressure crowded Rachel’s ribs, sharp and offended.

“This was the risk,” the older woman said. “You don’t redirect something like that without loss.”

Lena’s voice was tight. “You said it would bleed off.”

“It did,” the woman said. “Just not where you wanted.”

Miri sobbed once, short and startled, like the sound surprised her. “Make it stop.”

The pressure leaned forward.

Rachel felt the pull again, stronger now, like a current finding a new channel. Her hands lifted off her thighs before she noticed they were moving.

“No,” Lena said, sharp.

Rachel froze, fingers curled in midair.

The pressure pressed tighter, impatient.

Rachel dropped her hands back down, palms flat against her legs. Her heartbeat spiked, then smoothed too fast.

Miri screamed again, louder this time.

The chalk dust settled.

The older woman backed toward the seam and pressed her palm to the stone. “That’s enough.”

The room didn’t listen right away.

Then the pressure shifted, pulling back a fraction, like a tide hitting a shelf.

Miri collapsed forward, sobbing hard now, clutching her arm.

The bulbs steadied.

Silence fell, thick and wrong.

Rachel’s breath came shallow and quick. She stared at her hands, at how still they were.

“I can fix it,” she said.

Every head turned.

The older woman’s voice was flat. “No.”

Rachel swallowed. “It moved when she did. If I just—”

“No,” the woman said again, louder now. “You don’t fix things yet. You make them worse.”

The pressure crowded Rachel’s chest and stayed there, tight and aggrieved, like it had been denied something it wanted.

Lena crouched beside Miri, careful not to touch her arm. “We’ll get you stabilized.”

Miri shook her head, tears streaking down her face. “I don’t feel… in order.”

Rachel closed her eyes.

Behind them, the seam creaked softly.

The older woman watched Rachel from across the room, eyes sharp and tired. “This is why we stop the run,” she said. “Because motion hides the damage.”

Rachel opened her eyes.

The pressure leaned forward.

Not moving her.

Waiting.

# Chapter 24 — Miri First

They moved Miri first.

Not lifted. Not carried.

Someone slid a mat across the floor and Miri ended up on it in pieces, her body guided by hands that hovered instead of touched, fingers stopping short and then adjusting again when the first angle proved wrong. She cried under her breath, teeth knocking together, breath coming apart in little skips. Her arm lay against her side in a way that wasn’t broken and wasn’t right, the timing off, like it had missed a step and never caught up.

Rachel stayed in the chair.

She didn’t stand. She didn’t lean. She kept her hands flat on her thighs and her feet planted exactly where they were, even when the pressure crowded her ribs and urged movement—not direction, just motion, the suggestion of it.

The older woman spoke without looking at Rachel. “Stay seated.”

Rachel nodded.

“And don’t watch.”

Rachel dropped her gaze to the floor, to the chalk smear between her boots where someone had tried to wipe a line away and only made it thinner.

“And if something goes wrong—” The woman paused, listening to the room instead of finishing the sentence. “—you don’t step in.”

Rachel nodded again, throat tight.
