Rachel laughed once, short and broken, the sound coming back to her late, echoing as if someone else had done it, and they want you still settled into the space the laugh left behind.

She stared at the nearest shield, at her own warped reflection in the curved plastic. She looked wrong. She looked late.

“What happens,” she asked, breath tearing in and out, “if I can’t?”

No one answered. They didn’t negotiate. They adjusted.

A flat panel unfolded from a case, its grid of dim lights pulsing in patterns too clean for this room, two of them holding it like a shield and angling it toward her without stepping closer. The grid brightened and the pressure in her chest reacted instantly, tightening like a muscle bracing, her breath catching as the air thickened and the space stiffened as if the room itself had been ordered to hold still.

Her knees trembled. She lowered herself onto the bolted stool behind the desk, her body arriving in the sit late, hips dropping after the decision, spine catching up.

The grid eased. Shoulders loosened a fraction.

“Good,” someone said. “Maintain that.”

Her hands shook on her thighs, the tremor shrinking but not stopping, twitching in tiny delayed pulses, the pressure inside her staying tight and unsatisfied.

A radio crackled. “Subject located. Initiate containment corridor. Bring frame.”

Frame.

Her stomach dropped and “Please” slipped out before she could stop it, the word tasting wrong as soon as it left her mouth.

A shield tilted—assessment, not sympathy.

“Don’t make it worse,” someone said softly.

Ask the room slid through her before she could reject it, and she stared at the desk, the lamp, the cord, the small rectangle of shadow beneath it that didn’t line up with the light.

“No,” she whispered.

You already did, it said.

Her fingers twitched. The grid tightened. A boot slid a fraction on tile that had gone slick.

The room listened.

Rachel closed her eyes and didn’t reach for anything, didn’t picture moving the space, just shifted her weight inside her own body—forward, back—enough to be a choice, and the pressure snapped toward it as the lamp slid again, not far, just enough.

The grid stuttered, one light blinking out and coming back late as someone swore behind a shield.

“Stop—”

She opened her eyes. Nothing had resolved. The team was still there, still aiming, still trying to hold a space that refused to stay held, the pressure leaning closer, tight and interested, again threading through the moment without volume.

She didn’t move. The lamp slid again, cord dragging; a shield bumped a shelf and paper rasped, the sound arriving late and multiplying into dry static.

The grid flared, trying to correct, the air thickening until her lungs stuttered and heat breathed out of the wall, dust waking.

“Abort the grid!” someone shouted. “It’s feeding the desync!”

The panel went dark. The stiffness stayed.

Heavy boots gathered outside. The transfer team.

“Clear the archive,” a confident voice called from the hall. “We’re moving her.”

Too many bodies decided at once; relief and fear rippled, and the pressure tilted toward the doorway, drawn by cadence and the promise of a clamp—if they frame you again, it spills.

Rachel stood in pieces, legs straightening before her torso finished, balance arriving late and overshooting, hands lifting with palms open—not surrender, not threat, just visible.

“Don’t,” someone said, but the word didn’t carry.

She backed between shelves that felt closer than they had been a second ago, paper smelling sharp, ink and dust and old glue, hit a shelf that wasn’t there yet, swore as a folder slid off and hit the floor, the papery slap arriving half a beat after it landed.

The doorway filled with heavier armor, a frame on wheels unfolding behind them, metal whispering as rings aligned with clean, merciless geometry.

The room was too full.

Choose, it said.

“Where?” she whispered.

Nothing answered.

The frame finished unfolding.

Her vision slid sideways. Her weight shifted. The pressure snapped toward the movement like a held breath released as the archive light flickered and paper lifted in a slow, dry flutter, as if someone had exhaled through the shelves.

Rachel ran—not toward the door but into the narrow gap between two shelving units that should have been a dead end.

The space opened.

Not a door. Not a seam.

A mistake.

She fell through it shoulder-first, scraping skin and bone, breath knocked out of her as the world peeled away and the next one took too long to arrive. A shout followed her late and then cut off clean.

Rachel hit cold tile and rolled, lungs dragging for air.

The pressure inside her held tight.

Not guiding.  
Not apologizing.  
Waiting.

# Chapter 41 — The Wrong Floor

Rachel hit tile that wasn’t meant to be a floor.

Cold punched up through her shoulder and ribs as she rolled, skin scraping hard enough to burn, breath leaving her in a single useless rush that refused to come back right away. The world lagged behind the impact—sound arriving late, then all at once—metal clattering somewhere above her, paper tearing, a distant shout stretched thin like it had been pulled through water.

She lay there for half a second too long, lungs fluttering uselessly, chest spasming as if breathing had been rerouted and hadn’t found the new path yet.

Up, the pressure urged.

The word cut in without volume, not urgent, not kind, and her body obeyed before her head finished catching up, hands scrambling against tile that felt slicker than it looked. She pushed herself to a knee and nearly pitched forward again as gravity shifted sideways, the floor tilting just enough to be wrong.

Air rushed back into her chest in jagged pulls that burned. Her ankle screamed when she put weight on it, pain arriving late and then stacking, one signal tripping over the next.

The space around her did not resemble the archive.

No shelves. No paper smell. No desk.

The ceiling was lower here, unfinished concrete with seams that didn’t line up, fluorescent strips buzzing unevenly overhead, one flickering with a rhythm that didn’t match the others. The walls were bare except for conduit and a single painted line that ran waist-high and then vanished into nothing where the wall broke off into a corridor that bent sharply out of sight.
Her body read it as a ward and stayed on the safe side.

Rachel staggered upright and leaned against the wall, palm sliding over cold paint.

Above her—*above*—came noise.

Boots. Shouts. Orders snapping back and forth too quickly to separate. Metal whining as something heavy was moved into place. The sound of authority colliding with a space that had stopped agreeing.

Her heart hammered so hard it blurred her vision.

She took one step and the step landed twice.

The corridor ahead of her looked longer than it should have been, lights spaced unevenly, the far end dimming and brightening like it couldn’t decide whether to exist yet. A sign hung crooked from the ceiling by one bolt, swaying slightly, the letters stuttering as she tried to read them.

—MAINT—

The rest of the word arrived too late to matter.

Rachel limped forward, each step a negotiation with her ankle, with gravity, with the sense that the floor was listening for mistakes. Her hand brushed the wall for balance and left behind a faint smear of blood she didn’t remember earning.

Her breathing started to even out. Not because she calmed down—because the pressure inside her shifted, redistributing, pressing less on her chest and more along her spine, a presence that felt like it was settling in to watch.

Wrong level, it noted.

“What does that mean,” she whispered, voice hoarse, and the words came back to her a fraction late, echoing like someone else had spoken them.

No answer. Just the pressure holding steady.

She reached the corner and stopped short.

The corridor opened into a long maintenance bay filled with equipment that didn’t match—carts pushed half into walls, ladders bolted to nothing, a row of lockers dented and tagged with numbers that repeated out of sequence. The floor was scuffed with overlapping tracks that didn’t line up, some ending abruptly in the middle of the room like whatever made them had stopped existing.

A door at the far end stood ajar, light spilling out from behind it in a clean, confident rectangle that didn’t flicker.

Rachel stared at that light.

Her stomach twisted.

“That’s not—” she started.

Not for you, it said.

The pressure tightened slightly, enough to be felt, not enough to hurt.

She backed away from the door instead, turning down a narrower side passage that smelled faintly of oil and ozone. The lights here were dimmer, the hum uneven, one strip buzzing loud enough to set her teeth on edge.

Her foot caught on a seam in the floor that hadn’t been there a second earlier and she stumbled, catching herself on a railing that vibrated under her grip like it was carrying a current.

Pain flared up her arm, delayed and sharp.

She gasped and the sound snagged halfway out, throat closing reflexively as the pressure inside her surged in response.

Careful, it warned.

“Don’t,” she said, louder this time, and kept moving.

The passage sloped again, shallow but persistent, pulling her downward until her calves ached and her ankle screamed every time she tried to compensate. Sweat slicked her palms. Her heartbeat thudded out of sync with the lights, each pulse arriving a beat late.

The noises above her grew more distant.

Not gone. Redirected.

She reached another corner and slowed, breath coming in harsh pulls, vision swimming. The wall here was marked with faded symbols—arrows, hazard triangles, numbers crossed out and rewritten until they were illegible. Someone had scratched a word into the paint with something sharp and then tried to scrub it away.

The word came into focus and then slipped.

—DO—  
—NOT—  
—USE—

Rachel swallowed.

She took one more step and the floor dipped abruptly, not enough to be a fall but enough to steal her balance. She windmilled, grabbed at empty air, and slammed shoulder-first into a closed door that flexed under the impact.

The door didn’t open.

The wall behind it *did*.

Not breaking. Not collapsing.

Just… sliding.

A seam appeared where there hadn’t been one and widened with a soft, wet sound, concrete shifting aside like it had been waiting for permission. Cold air rushed out, smelling different—cleaner, sharper, like rain trapped underground.

Rachel froze, hand pressed against the door, heart hammering.

“No,” she whispered.

The opening widened another inch.

This is what you wanted, it said.

“I didn’t,” she said, voice shaking.

You wanted out of the frame.

Her stomach dropped.

Boots thundered somewhere above her again, closer now, the sound of pursuit reorganizing itself. A voice barked an order that made the corridor vibrate in response.

The opening in the wall was wide enough now to see into.

Not another hallway.

Not a room.

A space that didn’t finish resolving—edges blurring, light bending strangely, the floor inside slanting at an angle that didn’t correspond to gravity. Something inside it moved, not fast, not slow, just *out of phase* with the rest of the world.

Rachel backed away from it, shaking her head.

“No. No. No.”

The pressure inside her leaned forward, interested.

If they reach you here, it tears wider, it warned.

Her ankle screamed as she shifted her weight. She glanced back down the corridor she’d come from—empty now, lights flickering, the hum uneven—but the sound of boots was too close, echoing wrong, doubled.

She looked at the opening again.

Her breath hitched.

“Where does it go?” she asked, not expecting an answer.

The pressure tightened, almost approving.

Somewhere that won’t hold them, it answered.

Her heart slammed against her ribs.

The corridor lights flared bright and then dimmed, shadows jumping. A shape appeared at the far end of the passage—armor, reflective, too clean for this place.

Rachel made a sound that might have been a laugh or a sob and stepped backward.

The floor behind her shifted.

Not yet.
Waiting.

# Chapter 42 — What Slips Through

Rachel didn’t jump.

She stepped because her ankle failed first, because the floor behind her loosened its opinion about being solid, because the space in front of her felt crowded by intent even though nothing had touched her yet. Her heel crossed the seam and the moment stretched thin, tugged sideways, then let go all at once.

The floor didn’t drop so much as forget her.

She slid—half standing, half falling—cold air tearing up around her legs as the concrete edge skimmed her calf and vanished, the sound she made not matching fear so much as shock at being misplaced. Gravity hesitated. Her stomach did something unpleasant.

She hit something that bent and corrected.

Not soft.  
Not solid.

A surface that accepted her weight late and then pitched her sideways anyway, sending her shoulder into a wall that didn’t line up with anything else. Pain arrived out of order—ribs, then hip, then shoulder again as if it wanted a second try.

Rachel lay there with her face pressed to the floor, palms scraping against a texture that felt like tile until it didn’t, grain shifting under her fingers like the surface was still deciding what it was supposed to be.

Behind her, the opening closed.

Not a slam.  
A decision.

The sound came twice.

Her lungs stuttered. Breath scraped shallow and fast, each inhale catching like it had to pass through something narrow before it was allowed in.

Stay down, it urged—

The words crowded in too close, not comfort, not command, and she hated that her body listened anyway, muscles locking while her heart battered against her ribs like it wanted out.

“No,” she said, but the word slid out weak and wrong, and she stayed there because the idea of standing felt like it would peel her apart.

Light crept in uneven strips overhead, not fluorescent, not warm, something in between that made the shadows smear instead of settle. The walls curved in ways that kept slipping when she tried to track them, angles drifting just enough to make her dizzy.

This wasn’t a hallway.  
It wasn’t a room.

The air smelled sharp and sweet, ozone and something like rain trapped underground, and every sound she made came back altered, breath returning half a beat late, the scrape of her sleeve echoing longer than it should have.

She pushed herself up and immediately regretted it as her vision washed pale, pressure inside her chest shifting forward in response, attentive in a way that made her skin crawl.

Careful, it warned.

Her ankle throbbed, swelling fast, heat blooming out of proportion to the injury. She grabbed it without thinking, fingers shaking, the shaking refusing to stop even after she noticed it.

“Where are we,” she whispered.

The space bent the words before giving them back.

No answer. Just the pressure holding steady, closer now, like it had leaned in to watch.

She tried to stand and failed halfway, body arriving late to its own decision, knees buckling as the floor dipped again—not opening, not collapsing, just *misbehaving*. She caught herself on a low rail that ran along the wall, metal warm under her palm.

The rail pulsed.

Once.

Rachel yanked her hand back like she’d touched something alive.

It’s listening, it warned—

“Everything is listening,” she snapped, anger punching through the fear sharp enough to hurt, and limped forward anyway, hand skimming the wall for balance, counting steps without meaning to—one, two—then stopping when the numbers slipped and refused to line up.

The space widened ahead of her into something that felt almost polite by comparison, ceiling lifting, lights spacing themselves more evenly, grids and arcs painted across the floor that overlapped without agreeing, some faded, some too bright, like several versions of the room had been mapped on top of each other and left there.

At the center stood a structure.

Not a machine.  
Not furniture.

A frame, but wrong—thinner, incomplete, angles bending inward where they should have flared, cables sinking into the floor and disappearing into seams that pulsed faintly, out of sync with her heartbeat.

Rachel stopped short, breath snagging.

“No.”

The pressure inside her tightened, not warning, not reassurance.

Not built for holding, it said.

Her ears rang as she edged around it, ankle screaming every time she put weight down wrong, the light inside the structure lagging behind her movement, shadows adjusting late like they were embarrassed to be caught out.

Something shifted inside it.

Not solid.  
Not stable.

The air folded inward and relaxed again.

Rachel backed away and nearly lost her balance as a sound reached her from behind.

Footsteps.

Not boots.

Bare feet, slapping lightly against the floor in a rhythm that didn’t quite repeat.

She turned too fast, pain tearing up her leg, and had to grab the wall to stay upright as a figure stepped into view from behind a curve in the wall—half seen at first, then all at once.

A person.

Young. Or not. It was hard to tell.

Barefoot. Gray uniform worn thin at the edges. Hair cut close, eyes too bright, pupils blown wide like they’d spent too long in bad light.

They stopped when they saw her.

The moment stretched awkwardly.

“You’re not supposed to be here,” the person said, voice echoing wrong, splitting and rejoining.

Rachel swallowed, throat dry.

“I could say the same.”

The person’s mouth twitched, not quite a smile, not quite anything.

“They’ll come,” they said. “They always do.”

Rachel looked back at the wall behind her. Smooth. No seam. No sign it had ever opened.

“I can’t go back.”

“No one does.”

The pressure inside her chest sharpened, alert now.

This one slipped earlier, it noted.

Rachel’s breath hitched.

“You can hear it too?” she asked before she could stop herself.

The person’s eyes widened a fraction.

“Oh,” they said quietly. “That’s bad.”

The lights rippled in sequence, a wave moving through the space like a signal being passed along something she couldn’t see. The wrong frame pulsed brighter.

The pressure leaned forward, interested.

They found the seam, it said.

Rachel closed her eyes for half a second.

“Then we move,” she said.

The other person hesitated, gaze flicking to her ankle, the frame, the walls that wouldn’t settle.

“Move where?”

Rachel didn’t have an answer.

The hum deepened. Layers stacked out of sync. The space waited.

# Chapter 43 — Shared Weight

They didn’t move right away.

Rachel leaned into the wall because it was there and because the floor still felt undecided about whether standing counted as a stable request. The ache in her ankle had spread upward into a heavy, nauseating throb that made her leg feel borrowed, like it belonged to a slightly misaligned version of her body and hadn’t been returned properly.

A few steps away, the other person shifted their weight without settling, bare feet testing the floor in small, uncertain adjustments. They watched the space more than they watched Rachel, eyes tracking the wrong frame at the center of the chamber and then flicking away again, like looking at it too directly caused problems they’d learned not to invite.

The hum thickened.

Not louder.  
Denser.

It pressed against Rachel’s ears until the air felt packed, resistant.

The pressure pressed a flat statement into her head: you’re carrying excess variance.

Rachel swallowed. “That’s bad,” she muttered, not bothering to ask.

The other person’s head snapped up—not toward the sound, but toward *her*. “What?”

“Nothing,” Rachel said too quickly, and immediately hated how thin it sounded.

The floor twitched.

Not a shake.  
A correction.

Rachel’s shoulder slid against the wall with a soft scrape that echoed from two directions at once. The pressure inside her chest shifted sideways, searching, as if trying to find a position that fit and failing.

The other person rubbed their arms, fingers digging into skin hard enough to leave pale crescents. “When it starts doing that,” they said, nodding at the floor, “you don’t argue with it. You make yourself smaller. Or farther away.”

“From what?” Rachel asked.

They hesitated. “From the part that doesn’t belong.”

Rachel snorted once, sharp and breathless. “That’s reassuring.”

The lights flickered in response, one strip dimming while another overcorrected, brightness rippling unevenly across the ceiling.

The wrong frame answered.

Its internal distortion tightened, light folding inward and then slipping back, the air around it bending just enough to make Rachel’s eyes sting.

Weight is misallocated, it noted.

Rachel pushed off the wall experimentally and immediately regretted it. The floor dipped under her good foot—not instantly, but delayed, as if it had waited to be sure—stealing her balance after she thought she’d found it. She grabbed for the rail and felt a faint pulse under her palm, warmth bleeding into her skin.

The other person stepped closer without meaning to, reaching out and stopping short, hand hovering inches from Rachel’s elbow.

“Don’t touch,” they said sharply, eyes on the rail, not on Rachel.

Rachel froze.

The space reacted anyway.

A low groan rolled through the floor—not sound so much as pressure—like something large had shifted its weight somewhere below them. The painted lines on the floor shimmered, arcs slipping out of alignment and snapping back with a faint visual lag.

Rachel’s heart hammered.

You destabilize first, it said.

“That’s not a choice,” Rachel muttered.

The other person let out a short, humorless laugh. “It always is.”

Boots sounded somewhere distant.

Not above.  
Not below.

Close in a way that didn’t respect the geometry of the room.

Rachel felt it immediately—the pressure inside her tightening, attention snapping toward the sound like it had been waiting for confirmation.

They’re mapping stress through you, it warned.

The wrong frame pulsed brighter, cables sinking imperceptibly deeper into the floor, as if rooting.

The other person took a step back.

The floor under *their* feet responded differently—steadier, less delayed, like it preferred their weight. Rachel saw it happen and the noticing made everything worse.

Her ankle flared with pain, sharp and jagged, and the floor dipped harder beneath her this time, late but decisive, as if it had finally made up its mind.

The other person swore under their breath. “It’s pulling *you*,” they said, voice tight. “That’s not good.”

Rachel laughed once, breathless. “You’re saying that like it’s new.”

You are the variable.

The lights drained again, edges of the room smearing as if someone had dragged a thumb across reality. The hum deepened until it vibrated in her teeth.

The boots were closer now—individual steps audible, cadence breaking and reforming as whoever was moving recalibrated.

The other person widened the distance between them without looking back.

The floor steadied beneath them.

Rachel felt it like a rebuke.

“Hey,” she said, panic sharpening her voice. “Don’t—”

“I’ve done this before,” the other person said, jaw tight. “If you stay close, it collapses faster.”

“Done what?”

“Stayed.”

The wrong frame shuddered, its internal distortion collapsing inward for a heartbeat before surging back brighter, harsher.

They’re rerouting containment around you, it said.

Rachel pushed herself upright despite her ankle screaming, forcing weight through pain that arrived in overlapping waves. The floor rejected her this time—not opening, not breaking, but *pushing back*, like it resented the demand.

She staggered toward the other person, hand outstretched.

“Don’t leave me here,” she said, the words tearing out of her before she could stop them.

The other person hesitated, conflict flickering across their face.

The space held its breath.

Boots hit a nearby threshold.

A voice called out, distorted, stretched. “We’ve got movement. Brace.”

The wrong frame answered with a deep, resonant pulse that blurred Rachel’s vision.

Choose now, it said.

Rachel didn’t know what that meant.

She only knew the distance between them mattered, that the room was already negotiating which of them it could afford.

She took one more step.

The floor dipped.

The other person reached for her at the same instant the space lurched sideways.

Everything slid.

# Chapter 44 — Where It Lands

The slide didn’t stop so much as redistribute.

Rachel hit the floor hard on her side and kept moving, the surface beneath her shifting just enough to keep her from finding purchase, friction arriving late and uneven like the room was rationing it. Her shoulder screamed when it met resistance, pain blooming sharp and bright before splintering into smaller copies that chased each other down her arm.

She tasted blood.

The other person went down too, but not the same way—caught by the floor sooner, rolling once and stopping cleanly a few feet away, breath leaving them in a tight grunt that didn’t echo the way Rachel’s did.

The difference landed heavy.

Rachel lay there gasping, chest stuttering, vision swimming as the hum thickened until it felt like it was inside her skull instead of around it.

It took the hit and kept the mess, the voice said.

“That’s—” she tried, and the word dissolved into a cough that tore at her throat. Her ankle flared again as she shifted, pain stacking, heat blooming too fast.

The lights overhead flared and then dimmed, not flickering now but draining, edges of the space smearing as if someone had wiped reality with a damp cloth.

The other person pushed themselves up on one elbow and froze when they saw her.

“Don’t move,” they said, voice tight. “You slid too far.”

Rachel laughed once, thin and hysterical. “I didn’t exactly get a vote.”

The floor dipped under her hip, delayed and pointed, as if agreeing.

You went where it didn’t want you, it said.

Her stomach dropped.

“Stop saying things like that,” she snapped, though she knew it wouldn’t listen.

The wrong frame pulsed somewhere nearby, not in her direct line of sight but close enough that she could feel it through the soles of her feet, a low pressure beat that didn’t match her heart.

The other person crawled a step closer and then stopped, palm pressed flat to the floor like they were testing ice.

“Every time you move,” they said carefully, “it reacts harder. Like you’re—” They searched for the word and gave up. “Like you’re dragging something behind you.”

Rachel closed her eyes.

The pressure inside her shifted, not denying it.

You didn’t leave it behind.

Her breath came shallow and fast. “Then tell me how to get rid of it.”

The other person shook their head. “You don’t. You wait and hope it decides somewhere else is easier.”

“That’s not helpful.”

“No,” they agreed. “It’s just how this place works.”

Boots sounded again, closer now, the cadence distorted by whatever geometry they were passing through. A voice barked an order that arrived stretched and warped, syllables overlapping.

The hum deepened in response.

Rachel tried to roll onto her back and immediately regretted it as the floor dipped again, sharper this time, like a reprimand. She cried out before she could stop herself, the sound tearing free and coming back to her late, wrong.

The other person flinched and backed away instinctively.

The floor steadied under them.

Rachel felt the difference like a slap.

It likes you farther away, it observed.

“Oh, fuck you,” she whispered, tears blurring her vision. She wasn’t sure if she meant the voice, the room, or herself.

The lights dimmed further, shadows pooling in corners that hadn’t been there before. The wrong frame pulsed again, brighter now, and she could swear she felt something tug inside her chest in response, a pressure threading through her ribs like it was testing how much she’d give.

The other person’s jaw clenched. “They’re almost through,” they said. “When they breach, this place is going to panic.”

“Meaning?”

“Meaning it’ll grab the easiest thing and pull.”

Rachel dragged a breath in through clenched teeth. Her ankle throbbed in time with the hum, each pulse arriving just after the sound.

It won’t take the hard thing first, it warned.

Her heart stuttered.

“Say that again,” she demanded.

The pressure didn’t repeat itself. It only tightened, close and intent, like it was watching to see if she’d understood.

The other person looked between Rachel and the frame, calculation flickering across their face. “If I move farther away,” they said slowly, “it might settle.”

“No,” Rachel said immediately, panic spiking. “Don’t.”

They hesitated. “You heard what I said earlier. Staying close makes it worse.”

“I don’t care,” she shot back. “I’m not letting it choose for me.”

The floor shifted under her, a shallow dip that made her gasp.

It already has.

Boots hit the edge of the space, loud now, real, the sound snapping into place as if they’d finally found the right angle. A shadow moved across the wall, armor catching the dim light.

The wrong frame answered with a violent pulse that knocked the air from Rachel’s lungs. She cried out, clutching at her chest as the pressure spiked, sharp and invasive, like something had hooked under her ribs and pulled.

The other person shouted her name—too late, the sound stretched and torn as the space lurched again.

Rachel felt herself sliding, not across the floor this time but *into* it, the surface thinning beneath her spine like ice under weight.

“No,” she sobbed, and then there was no floor.

# Chapter 45 — After the Floor

There was weight and then there wasn’t, and the difference hurt more than the fall.

Rachel dropped through cold that had shape to it, edges catching at her clothes, her body turning without asking her to keep up. Something brushed her shoulder and slid away when she grabbed at it, fingers closing on nothing that stayed. Sound vanished mid-motion—no rush, no scream—just the pressure of going without air agreeing to be there.

Then sound came back wrong.

A low rushing, like breath dragged through a crack too small for it, followed by a wet thud that knocked the air from her chest when she hit again. This surface didn’t bend. It answered hard and stayed that way, pain blooming across her back and ribs and refusing to break apart into manageable pieces.

She lay still because moving required permission she didn’t have.
No answering voice followed her down.

The air tasted used. Not old. Handled. Like something had breathed it first and decided not to give it back.

Her lungs worked in shallow, scraping pulls. When she twitched her ankle by accident, pain flared bright enough to blur the edges of the dark and she bit down on the inside of her cheek until copper filled her mouth.

The dark wasn’t whole.

Light slid through in thin bands that didn’t agree on direction, crawling across uneven surfaces, catching on walls that didn’t quite meet the floor and a ceiling that sagged in places like it had given up pretending.

Rachel rolled onto her side and immediately regretted it, the motion dragging pain through her spine like a hooked wire. She gasped and pressed her face to the ground, grit biting into her skin, the surface warm in one place and cold an inch away.

You’re still in one piece, it said.

She laughed once, breathless and ugly. “Lucky me.”

The pressure didn’t move away.

This is where it puts what doesn’t fit, it said.

Her stomach clenched.

She whispered, “What,” and the word smeared, stretched thin when it came back to her, not quite hers anymore.

Something shifted.

Not close.  
Not far.

Just present.

Rachel froze so hard her muscles trembled. The sound wasn’t footsteps. It dragged, paused, dragged again, like effort was being considered and reconsidered between movements.

Her heart hammered loud enough she was sure it could hear it.

Not yet, it warned.

She didn’t know what that meant, but her body obeyed, breath caught halfway in, chest burning.

The light slid again and caught on something a few feet away—a section of wall peeled back to show layers underneath, concrete giving way to something softer that pulsed faintly, like it remembered having a rhythm once.

The dragging stopped.

Rachel counted breaths because counting felt safer than thinking. One scraped in. The next caught. The third hurt worse.

It knows you’re there, it said.

“No,” she breathed, the word barely sound.

The dark rearranged itself.

A shape pulled free from the floor where the light didn’t quite reach. Not a person. Not an absence either. It moved by folding itself forward, compressing and releasing in increments that didn’t match distance or time.

Rachel’s eyes slid off it. Looking straight made her dizzy, like the center of it refused to stay where she put it.

Don’t look straight, it warned.

She didn’t understand why, only that obeying made the thing smaller somehow—not clearer, just less everything at once. Too many joints. Not enough edges. A surface that caught light and then let it go like it hadn’t decided whether it wanted it.

It paused.

The pressure leaned closer.

It’s deciding if you’re worth it, it said.

Rachel swallowed a sound.

The thing dragged part of itself forward with a noise like fabric pulled across grit, then stopped again, angled wrong, attention snagging on her ankle.

Her pulse roared.

Pain shows, it warned.

She clamped her hand over her mouth, nails digging in.

The light above flared suddenly, bright enough to sting, and the thing recoiled, compressing inward like it had been surprised. It retreated a fraction, movements jerky and irritated, as if the interruption offended it.

Rachel didn’t wait to understand why.

She dragged herself backward in inches, palms scraping skin away, breath coming in ragged pulls. The ground resisted in places and then released without warning, throwing off her timing, making every movement feel like a mistake she hadn’t finished making yet.

Slow, it urged.

She froze again, heart trying to claw its way out.

The thing shifted, then settled, attention sliding away from her like an animal losing interest when the effort outweighed the reward.

Rachel sagged against the wall and let out a breath that shook.

Her body wouldn’t stop trembling.

The pressure eased—not gone, just less forward, like it had leaned back to watch.

This place forgets what stops moving, it said.

Tears leaked out despite her clenched jaw.

“Good,” she whispered. “I’ll practice.”

The light dimmed.

Somewhere not-here, something heavy moved, a distant groan rippling through layers of space that didn’t line up.

Rachel stayed very still and waited to see what would remember her next.

# Chapter 46 — Not Where It Keeps Things

The light changed first.

Not brighter. Not darker.  
Different.

Rachel felt it through her eyelids before she opened them, a pressure shift that didn’t belong to the place she was in, like someone had opened a door somewhere far above and the air had decided to notice. The thing near the wall stilled, its shape compressing in on itself, attention snapping away from her so fast it felt like being dropped.

The pressure inside her recoiled.

That’s not meant to be open, it warned.

Before she could ask what *that* meant, the floor lurched.

Not a slide.  
A failure.

The surface beneath her thinned all at once, no warning, no negotiation, and Rachel screamed as gravity returned with teeth, hauling her down and then up in the same breath, the sensation so wrong it turned her stomach inside out.

The dark tore.

Light punched through in a jagged column, white and hard and real, and the space around her folded inward like it was trying to close a wound. Something grabbed at her ankle—pressure, not hands—and then let go with a sharp, irritated snap.

You’re in the way now, it said.

She hit ground that did not move.

Grass. Gravel. Something wet and cold.

Rachel rolled hard, breath exploding out of her lungs, shoulder slamming into earth that smelled alive and filthy and right. She lay there choking, palms sinking into mud, the night air cutting into her skin like it had edges.

Rain hit her face.

Not a memory.  
Not a projection.

Actual rain, cold and stinging, soaking through her clothes in seconds.

Above her, something boomed.

Not thunder.

Metal screaming under stress. A distant crack like concrete failing wrong. Shouts followed—human voices, panicked and overlapping, words she couldn’t separate but recognized as real because they didn’t smear when they hit her ears.

The pressure inside her chest pulled tight, disoriented.

Outside isn’t quieter, it warned.

“I don’t care,” she gasped, dragging air into her lungs like she’d been underwater too long. Her ankle screamed when she tried to move, pain sharp and grounding, proof she was back in a body that obeyed physics even when it hated her.

She rolled onto her side and vomited into the grass, bile burning her throat, hands shaking so badly she couldn’t keep herself upright afterward. Rain plastered her hair to her face. Mud coated her palms and knees.

The world stayed put.

Lights flared somewhere nearby—harsh, industrial, mounted too high—and silhouettes moved through them, figures running, slipping, shouting orders that overlapped and collided.

Someone yelled her name.

Or maybe just a name.

The pressure inside her shifted, retreating, like it had been shoved out of the room and was deciding whether to try the door again.

They broke something important, it said.

Rachel laughed once, wet and hysterical, and pressed her forehead into the mud.

“Good,” she whispered.

Behind her, the ground shuddered.

Not collapsing.  
Sealing.
Below, whatever had opened sealed. Above, the outer doors must have been thrown wide for evacuation.

A deep, final sound rolled through the earth, like a vault door closing somewhere she couldn’t see. The lights steadied. The shouting changed pitch, urgency reorganizing itself.

Hands grabbed her shoulders.

Rachel flinched violently, gasping, swinging blind, nails scraping against fabric and skin until someone cursed and let go.

“Easy—easy—don’t—”

She curled in on herself instinctively, rain slicking her lashes, heart hammering so hard she thought it might tear free.

The pressure inside her didn’t answer.

Not gone.

Just… pushed back.

Rachel lay there in the mud and rain and noise and let the outside world prove itself by continuing to exist.

She was free of the space.

Whatever else had come with her was going to have to keep up.

Rachel stayed down longer than she meant to.

Rain slid into her hair and down the side of her face, cold enough to sting, steady enough to count. Her ankle throbbed in a slow, ugly rhythm that felt solid, dependable in a way nothing else did. She pressed her palm into the mud and pushed, stopping halfway when the ground dipped under her weight and then settled again like it had changed its mind.

She breathed anyway.

In.  
Out.  
Not evenly.

“Are you—” Her voice cracked and she swallowed hard. “Are you still there.”

Nothing answered right away.

The pressure sat just behind her ribs, not gone, not forward, like something that had leaned back in its chair.

“Did anything come with me,” she whispered, because that felt like the most important question and also the one she least wanted answered.

Rain filled the space where a response should have been. Somewhere nearby, a light buzzed and cut out and buzzed back on.

Her jaw tightened.

“What did they break,” she said, sharper now, words pushed out without care.

The pressure shifted, late.

The voice slid in: you didn’t wait—

“For what,” she snapped, and the anger surprised her with how easy it came.

She wiped rain out of her eyes and forced herself to look properly. Grass torn to mud. A slope that didn’t line up with how she remembered the ground being. A fence farther off, leaning wrong, reflective tape snapping wetly in the wind. Below the tape, thin tags fluttered with knot-stamped warnings instead of words.
A bell rang somewhere down the slope, two slow notes that meant shelter.

Footprints.

Not hers.

Too many. Too light. Coming toward her instead of away.

Her stomach dropped.

“That’s wrong,” she murmured, and then louder, “No one else was—”

“Rachel?”

The sound hit her late.

She flinched hard, twisting toward it, pain tearing up her leg as she moved too fast. The world slid and then caught.

Lena stood a few yards away.

Not stepping into view.  
Just there.

Rain darkened her jacket. Her hair was plastered to her forehead. She was breathing hard, chest rising and falling too fast, like she’d been running longer than she should have. Mud already coated her shoes.

For a second, Rachel couldn’t make the image settle.

Lena wasn’t supposed to be here.

Not outside.  
Not now.  
Not past the doors.

“How are you out here,” Rachel blurted, the words coming out wrong, accusation bleeding through fear before she could stop it.

Lena blinked. “What?”

“How did you get out here,” Rachel said again, pushing herself upright despite her ankle screaming. The ground dipped and steadied and she ignored it, eyes locked on Lena. “I thought everything was sealed.”

Lena took a step closer and then stopped like she’d hit an invisible line. Confusion flickered across her face. “Rachel, I heard something break and then—” She waved a hand behind her, the gesture incomplete. “People started running. The outer doors were open. I just— I followed.”

Rachel shook her head, slow and small. “That doesn’t line up,” she said. “You shouldn’t have been able to just walk out.”

Rain drummed between them.

The pressure leaned in, curious.

She isn’t where you left her, it noted.

Rachel’s breath hitched.

Lena reached out and then pulled her hand back, fingers curling into her sleeve. “You’re bleeding,” she said instead. “We need to get you up.”

Rachel looked down like that was new information. Mud on her hands. Blood smeared along her sleeve she didn’t remember earning. Her hands shaking hard enough that she had to clench them to make it stop.

“Lena,” she said quietly, because loud felt dangerous now. “How did you get past the doors.”

Lena opened her mouth.

Metal clanged behind them—loud, wrong—followed by raised voices snapping into urgency, boots hitting pavement out of sync.

Lena’s head turned toward the sound.

Rachel didn’t move.

The pressure slid back, repositioning, like it had lost interest in the question and was already looking ahead.

You don’t get to finish this, it said.

Lena’s hand landed on Rachel’s shoulder.

The moment tore.

# Chapter 47 — The Way She Points

Rachel takes a step before she means to.

The ground slopes just enough to make it feel like momentum instead of choice, and Lena tightens her grip automatically, adjusting them both without asking. Rachel’s ankle flares hot and bright and she hisses through her teeth, fingers digging into Lena’s sleeve.

“Careful,” Lena says. “You’re going to—”

“I know,” Rachel snaps, though she doesn’t. The world feels a half-beat late again, sound lagging behind motion, rain touching her skin before she sees it fall.

They stop near the edge of the road where the pavement breaks and turns to churned mud. The lights behind them are louder now, closer, but still wrong—voices overlapping without shape, urgency without direction.

Lena looks back once, then forward. “We should get off the road.”
“Find cover,” Rachel says. “A roof. Anything that isn’t open.”

Rachel follows her gaze.

Trees crowd in close on the left, dark and wet and thick with undergrowth. On the right, the road curves away, narrowing, dipping out of sight.

Lena points left. “That way. It’ll hide us.”

The pressure inside Rachel tightens, sharp enough to make her breath catch.

She doesn’t know that, the voice said.

Rachel swallows. “How do you know.”

Lena blinks. “Know what?”

“That it hides us.”

Lena’s mouth opens, closes. “I don’t. I just—Rachel, look at us. We can’t stay here.”

The rain ticks against the leaves like something counting.

Rachel stares into the trees. The dark there feels busy, layered, full of small sounds that don’t line up. The road, for all its exposure, at least stays straight for a few yards before it bends.

She points where things gather, it added.

“That’s not—” Rachel stops herself. Her heart is beating too fast, every thud arriving a fraction late. “You don’t know what’s in there.”

Lena exhales hard. “Neither do you.”

“I know it’s not empty.”

The words come out before Rachel can stop them. Lena looks at her, confusion sharpening into concern.

“Rachel,” she says carefully, “you’re not making sense.”

The pressure leans closer, almost pleased.

She wasn’t there when you fell, it said.

Rachel’s gaze snaps back to Lena. “You didn’t see it.”

“See what?”

“The way out,” Rachel says. “You didn’t see where I came from.”

Lena shifts her weight, uncomfortable. “I saw you on the ground. Bleeding. That was enough.”

“That’s not what I meant.”

Behind them, a light sweeps briefly across the road and then disappears again, too far away to belong to anyone paying attention.

Lena points left again, firmer this time. “We go now, or we don’t go at all.”

Rachel looks at Lena’s hand, at the way her finger doesn’t shake, at the certainty of the gesture.

She’s already chosen without you, it noted.

Something cold settles in Rachel’s stomach.

“If we go that way,” Rachel says slowly, “and it’s wrong—”

“It won’t be,” Lena says, too fast.

Rachel pulls her arm free.

The movement costs her. Pain spikes up her leg and she nearly stumbles, catching herself on nothing, breath tearing out of her in a sharp sound that makes Lena reach for her again.

Rachel steps back.

“Don’t,” she says.

Lena freezes. “What are you doing.”

Rachel turns to the road. The curve ahead is dark, wet, empty. It doesn’t promise safety. It doesn’t hide anything.

But it doesn’t close in either.

She won’t follow you there, it warned.

Rachel’s pulse roars in her ears.

“You don’t know that,” she whispers.

Lena looks from Rachel to the road, then back to the trees. “Rachel. Please.”

Rachel takes one step toward the road.

The world steadies. Just a fraction. Enough to notice.

She takes another.

“Rachel,” Lena says again, sharper now.

Rachel doesn’t answer. She limps forward, each step deliberate, wrong in a way that feels intentional. The road curves and takes her with it, pulling her line of sight away from the lights, from the trees, from the place Lena is standing.

She hears Lena hesitate behind her, then footsteps following.

Rachel doesn’t turn around.

# Chapter 48 — The Easy Way

Rachel’s foot comes down and the ground answers it cleanly, no slip, no hollow delay, and the wrongness of that makes her stumble anyway, breath hitching as her ankle flares hot and bright. She waits for the rest of it—for the lag, the sway, the world deciding whether to keep her upright—but it doesn’t come. The road holds. It keeps holding.

She blinks and takes another step, slower this time, and it still behaves.

Lena is close enough that Rachel can feel her through the damp fabric of her jacket, warmth and weight lining up in a way that shouldn’t matter and somehow does. Lena’s breathing is steady at her shoulder, human and ordinary, and the rain sounds like rain again instead of static tearing at the edges of Rachel’s hearing. The night pulls itself into something continuous.

Rachel swallows.

She shifts her weight without thinking and the road smooths under her foot like it was already expecting the move. The fence doesn’t drift. The curve ahead doesn’t warp when she looks at it too long. The trees stay where trees are supposed to stay.

“That—” Her voice catches. She clears her throat and tries again. “Did you feel that.”

Lena doesn’t answer. Her arm tightens reflexively, not guiding, just there, and the world seems to settle around the contact, pain blurring down from sharp to manageable, edges softening.

The pressure wakes up close and irritated.

Don’t get used to that, it warned.

Rachel almost laughs, the sound brittle in her chest. “Too late.”

She tries to step half a pace ahead, just to test it, and the ground grabs her too soon, jarring her ankle hard enough that she gasps. Lena catches her instantly, fingers digging in with more force than necessary.

“Slow down,” Lena says. “You’re rushing.”

The road clicks back into place and they start again, the path opening where Lena angles them, not dramatically, just enough—fewer stones, fewer dips, shadows thinning as if brushed aside.

Rachel notices without wanting to.

“You didn’t even look,” she says.

Lena glances down, confused. “Look at what.”

“The ground.”

Lena shrugs. “Didn’t need to.”

The pressure presses harder, unpleasant now.

She assumes, it noted.

Rachel stumbles again, smaller this time, and Lena steadies her before she can decide whether she wants it. The world obliges immediately, pain dulling, the air smoothing, the night loosening its grip.

Rachel exhales and doesn’t like the sound it makes. “Why is it better,” she mutters.

Lena frowns. “Because you’re not fighting it.”

That lands wrong in a way Rachel can’t quite isolate.

They reach a place where the road changes its mind—not a fork, just a widening, one side dipping and cluttered, the other open and clean and easy to follow. Lena doesn’t slow. She angles them toward the open side without comment, certainty in the motion.

The pressure spikes, sharp and immediate.

That way keeps you quiet, it warned.

Rachel digs her heel in on instinct. The world lurches, not collapsing, just slipping out of alignment enough to hurt, pain snapping bright up her leg, vision whitening at the edges.

Lena tightens her grip. “Stop doing that,” she says, breath sharp now. “You’re going to make it worse.”

The road steadies.

Rachel lets out a thin, broken laugh. “You feel that,” she says. “You have to.”

“I feel you shaking,” Lena says, frustrated, scared.

The pressure hums, displeased.

She likes when it’s easy, it said.

Rachel pulls her arm free and everything stutters—not enough to drop her, just enough to make the night feel loud again, rain too present, ground honest and unforgiving under her foot. She sways, catching herself on nothing, breath sawing out of her chest. Lena reaches for her and stops when Rachel shakes her head.

“Don’t,” Rachel says. “Just wait.”

The open road ahead looks inviting now, smooth and reasonable, the kind of choice that explains itself. The other way looks worse—narrow, uneven, shadows piled thick where the light doesn’t reach.

The pressure doesn’t rush her this time.

You can walk where it hurts, or you can walk where you disappear, it offered.

Rachel closes her eyes for half a second and steps toward the worse ground. Pain answers immediately, loud and honest, the night pressing back in, rain sharp in her ears again.

But it stays real.

Behind her, Lena exhales, frustrated and frightened. “Rachel. This is harder.”

Rachel nods, jaw clenched. “I know.”

She keeps moving, choosing discomfort over ease, and after a beat she hears footsteps again—not steadying, not leading.

# Chapter 49 — Too Close

They hear him before they see him.

A footstep that doesn’t belong to either of them, heavier, more deliberate, the sound of someone who expects to be heard and expects that to be enough. Rachel’s shoulders lock instantly, her body reacting before her thoughts catch up, the ringing still faint but present behind her eyes like pressure waiting for an excuse.

“Hey,” a voice calls out, close. Too close already. “You can’t—”

Lena’s hand tightens at Rachel’s back. Not steering. Warning.

Rachel turns.

The man is standing in the open stretch of road behind them, rain slicking his jacket dark, one hand half-raised in a gesture that’s meant to calm, meant to stop them without chasing. He looks confused more than angry, breath fogging, eyes flicking from Rachel’s ankle to Lena’s face.

“You need to stop,” he says. “This area’s restricted.”

The word lands wrong.

Rachel feels it like a hook behind her ribs, a tug backward toward explanation, toward slowing down, toward letting this moment stretch long enough to trap them.

Her chest tightens.

“No,” she says, and it isn’t loud.

The man steps forward anyway.

Just one step.  
Just enough.

Something in Rachel snaps into alignment—not thought, not fear, but refusal so sharp it feels clean.

Not you.  
Not now.  
Not this close.

She doesn’t raise her hands. She doesn’t brace. She doesn’t do anything that looks like action.

The world simply chooses distance.

The man’s foot comes down where the ground should be and doesn’t arrive with the rest of him. His body lurches forward without momentum, like the step forgot what it was supposed to do, and he stumbles hard, arms flailing as time catches up all at once.

He hits the road on one knee with a sharp, surprised sound, palms slapping wet pavement.

Rachel gasps.

The ringing slams into her skull a half-second later, high and sudden, a thin screaming tone that slices straight through her thoughts and makes the world tilt without moving. She squeezes her eyes shut, teeth clenched, the sound crowding everything else out.

There it is, the voice said.

The voice is right there now.

Not distant.  
Not restrained.

Bright with it.

You felt that, it said.  
You didn’t wait.

Rachel staggers, one hand flying up to her head, the ringing spiking as she moves. “Shut up,” she breathes, though she isn’t sure if the words leave her mouth or just bounce around inside her skull.

Lena swears under her breath and grabs Rachel’s arm. “Rachel—what did you—”

“I didn’t,” Rachel says, too fast. “I didn’t do anything.”

Behind them, the man pushes himself up, blinking hard like he’s trying to get his balance back, confusion etched deep across his face. “What the hell—”

The ringing surges again, pulsing in time with Rachel’s heartbeat.

Again, if you want, it offered.

Her stomach drops.

Lena pulls her, sharp and urgent now. “We’re leaving. Now.”

Rachel doesn’t argue.

They turn and move, Lena half-dragging her this time, feet slipping on wet ground, breath tearing out of Rachel’s chest in ragged pulls. The road bends and takes them, the man’s voice rising behind them, words blurring into noise she can’t separate from the ringing.

“What happened?” Lena demands, low and tight. “He just—he wasn’t even touching you.”

“I know,” Rachel says, and the truth of it makes her throat close. “I know.”

The ringing doesn’t fade. It modulates, sharpens when she hesitates, softens when she commits to forward motion, like something tuning itself to her.

You made space, it said.  
That’s all this ever was.

Rachel stumbles and Lena catches her, reality steadying around the contact even as her head screams. She hates how that works now. Hates how quickly the world behaves when she lets someone else anchor her.

They cut off the road and into the darker stretch, branches clawing at their clothes, the night loud again, uneven, honest. Behind them, someone shouts her name—maybe the man, maybe someone else—but the sound comes late, stretched thin.

The ringing peaks and then settles into a steady, invasive whine.

Rachel presses her fingers hard against her temples and keeps moving.

The voice stays with her, close and pleased and no longer pretending to be patient.

You’re allowed to do that now, it said.

She doesn’t answer.

She just runs.

# Chapter 50 — Still Ringing

They don’t stop all at once.

Lena slows first, breath rasping, hands braced on her knees like she’s holding herself together by force, and Rachel keeps moving another few steps before she realizes she’s doing it, ankle screaming every time it hits wrong ground. The trees thin into a narrow break where the rain doesn’t reach as hard, branches bending low enough to make the air feel pressed down.

“Okay,” Lena pants. “Okay—just—give me a second.”

Rachel stays upright because sitting feels like letting something arrive. The ringing is thinner now but constant, a high wire pulled tight behind her eyes, vibrating whenever she shifts her weight.

The world holds, uneven and loud, real enough to trust.

She presses her palms hard against her temples, as if pressure might cancel pressure.

You’re listening now, the voice says, right where the sound is worst.

Rachel drops her hands. “I didn’t ask you to stay.”

A flicker of amusement ripples through the ringing.

You didn’t ask me to leave, it said.

“That’s not—” She swallows, breath catching. “That’s not how this works.”

A pause. Curious. Interested.

Isn’t it, it said.

Lena straightens slowly, eyes fixed on Rachel’s face now instead of the ground. “Who are you talking to.”

“No one.” The word comes too fast. Rachel hates that. “My head.”

Lena watches her for a beat longer than necessary, rain ticking softly through leaves around them, then nods like she’s packing it away. “You scared him back there.”

Rachel’s stomach tightens. “I didn’t touch him.”

“I know,” Lena says. “He just… missed himself.”

The ringing sharpens, pleased.

She noticed, it said.

Rachel turns her head away, staring into the dark between the trees where the light doesn’t reach properly. “We need to keep moving.”

They do, slower now, careful, feet sliding on wet leaves and roots. Every few steps Rachel feels the world hesitate, like it’s waiting to see if she’ll change her mind.

“I don’t want to do that again,” she mutters, not sure if she’s talking to Lena or the sound in her head.

You will, the voice says immediately, too certain.

Her skin crawls. “You don’t know that.”

The ringing tightens, bright and eager, threading into the back of her thoughts.

You already crossed it, it said.

Rachel stops without meaning to. Her breath catches halfway in and stays there, chest burning as she stares too hard at the space ahead of her, like she can force it to make sense if she just looks long enough.

Lena slows beside her, watching Rachel instead of the trail now. “Rachel,” she says carefully, “you just stopped. What are you thinking about.”

“Nothing.” The lie tastes flat.

They stand there a beat too long, rain whispering, the space between them suddenly fragile in a way Rachel can’t explain.

The trail ahead barely exists, just a suggestion pressed through wet undergrowth. Lena hesitates, looking left, then right, uncertainty flickering across her face.

Rachel steps forward.

The ground firms under her foot, roots flattening just enough to hold, and the ringing softens, almost approving, like something settling into place.

Lena’s eyes flick to Rachel’s face, not the ground. “Did you just—”

“No.” Rachel cuts it off, jaw tight. She forces herself to stop. The earth stops helping immediately, goes back to being just earth, slick and uneven and uncooperative.

They stand there, the night breathing around them.

“Back there,” Lena says after a moment, “when he came toward us… you didn’t flinch.”

Rachel laughs once, brittle. “I did. Inside.”

“That’s not what I meant.”

The ringing presses closer, intimate.

She’s asking the wrong question, it said.

Rachel winces. “Stop.”

Lena frowns. “What.”

“Nothing.” Rachel rubs her face hard, smearing rain and dirt across her cheeks. “If someone else comes near us, don’t try to step in front of me.”

Lena stiffens. “Rachel—”

“I need space,” Rachel says, the words stacked too tightly together. “If they get close, let them.”

Lena studies her, fear and trust tangling openly. “You’re asking me to do nothing.”

“I’m asking you not to fix it.”

The ringing hums, pleased again.

She understands more than you think, it said.

Rachel starts forward, slower now, deliberate. The trail resists, then gives, the world not quite helping, not quite fighting her either.

The ringing never fully fades.

It stays with her, thin and eager, threaded through every step, waiting for the next time she decides not to wait.

# Chapter 51 — Not Waiting

The ground doesn’t give her anything this time.

It isn’t hostile, exactly, but it stops cooperating, roots catching her bad ankle after she’s already leaned into the step, slopes tilting late so her weight lands wrong and pain snaps sharp enough to make her breath hitch. The ringing tightens with it, not louder so much as closer, threading itself behind her eyes in a way that makes every misstep feel deliberate.

Lena slows, glancing back over her shoulder. “Do you want to rest?”

“No,” Rachel says, already moving again, because stopping feels like opening a door she can’t see. The night presses in, wet and close, rain slipping through the leaves in thin, persistent lines. The world holds together just enough to keep her upright, uneven and loud and real in a way she doesn’t trust.

She shifts her weight and the ringing flares, a thin white pressure that makes her vision fuzz at the edges.

You’re doing it again, the voice says, not accusing, just interested.

Rachel catches herself on a branch, bark scraping skin from her palm. “Doing what,” she mutters, breath coming too fast.

The answer doesn’t come right away. It slides in with the ringing, timed to the next wrong step.

Stopping before you have to, it said.

“I’m injured,” she snaps, not looking at Lena, not slowing. “That’s not the same thing.”

The ground dips late and her ankle screams, heat flashing bright enough that she has to grit her teeth to keep moving. The ringing spikes with it, then eases, like it’s learned something useful.

They break out of the trees onto the road without meaning to, the space opening too suddenly, sound stretching farther than it should. Rachel’s chest tightens before she knows why.

Lena slows. “Someone could see us.”

“I know.”

Rachel keeps walking.

The ringing smooths just a fraction, enough to notice.

You know how to keep space now, it said.

Her stomach drops. “Don’t.”

I didn’t say use it, it added.

“That’s worse,” she says, because it is.

Something shifts at the far end of the road, a shape moving where there shouldn’t be one yet, and the memory of footsteps closes around her ribs. Lena’s hand tightens on her arm, a reflex she doesn’t even seem to notice.

“I see it,” Rachel says, stopping without meaning to.

The ringing tightens instantly, bright and alert, threading straight through her skull.

That moment, the voice says, close enough to feel. Right before.

The figure takes a step closer. Still wrong. Still distant.

“I don’t want to hurt anyone,” Rachel says, and the words feel thin even to her.

The voice hums, indulgent.

You didn’t, it said.  
You made space.

“That doesn’t mean—” She swallows. “You don’t know that.”

They stayed behind, it said.

The figure moves again and Lena shifts beside her, ready to do something human and useless, to step in front, to slow this down, to negotiate.

The choice opens sharp and immediate in Rachel’s chest.

Wait.  
Or decide.

The ringing warms, insistent, almost encouraging.

You don’t have to wait for them to be closer, it urged.

Rachel jerks sideways instead, pulling Lena with her off the road and back into the trees. The ground fights it, slick and uneven, catching her foot hard enough that pain snaps bright up her leg and she hisses through her teeth.

The ringing spikes, sharp and irritated.

That’s slower, it said.

“I don’t care,” Rachel says, breath tearing out of her.

Branches claw at them as they push into the dark, sound folding back in on itself, the road disappearing behind leaves and rain. She doesn’t look back, but she feels the hesitation behind them, the wrong pause where pursuit should be.

She keeps moving until her lungs burn and her vision swims, leaning into a tree when her legs finally refuse to cooperate.

The ringing settles into a tight, dissatisfied whine.

You could have ended that already, it said.

Rachel presses her forehead to wet bark, chest heaving. “I’m not doing this for you.”

The pause is longer this time. Closer.

Of course not, it said.  
You’re doing it because it works.

Lena’s hand finds her shoulder, solid and warm. “We need to keep going,” she says quietly. “Next time—just tell me what you need.”

Rachel nods without opening her eyes.

The ringing stays.

Not loud.  
Not fading.

Just waiting.

# Chapter 52 — Somewhere That Holds

They keep moving without saying why, the kind of motion that doesn’t feel chosen so much as required, like breathing through pain until it dulls into something you can carry. The trees thin and thicken again, ground changing under Rachel’s feet—mud to gravel to something harder she doesn’t want to look at too closely—and the ringing stays tucked behind her eyes, not gone, just quieter, like it’s learned where to sit without being noticed.

“We can’t just stay out here,” Lena says eventually, breath rough, words practical in the way people get when they’re too tired to be scared properly.

Rachel nods without really knowing what she’s agreeing to. The idea of inside presses at her ribs anyway, images stacking up without permission—walls too close, doors that decide when they open, rooms that expect her to stay once she enters them.

“Somewhere dry,” Lena adds. “Somewhere we can lock.”

Rachel swallows, the ringing tightening just enough to be felt. “I don’t want to lock anything.”

“That’s what doors are for,” Lena says, tired, reasonable.

They crest a small rise and the trees fall back, revealing a cluster of buildings below, half-lit and uneven, lights burning in a few windows while others sit dark and hollow like missing teeth. It isn’t big enough to be a town or small enough to be nothing, just a place people stopped agreeing about all at once and never quite finished leaving.

The ringing shifts.

Not sharp.  
Not loud.

Interested.

Rachel slows before she means to, her body already resisting, and the ground steadies under her foot anyway, firming just enough to make the hesitation obvious. She hates that part most.

Lena notices, of course. “You see something?”

“No.” Rachel forces herself forward, steps careful but committed, pain snapping bright through her ankle and fading again. “I just don’t like it.”

“That’s everywhere,” Lena says, but the words don’t land with much confidence.

As they descend, the air changes—not temperature, but weight. Sound dulls slightly, rain quieter against surfaces that haven’t decided what they’re made of anymore. Rachel’s skin prickles, nerves stretched thin, tuned outward and inward at the same time.

They reach the edge of the buildings and she understands too late what’s wrong.

Nothing reacts.

No windows brighten. No movement answers theirs. Even the night feels distracted here, like it has better things to do than keep track of who comes and goes.

The ringing eases.

Not relief.  
Approval.

Lena exhales. “Okay. This is good. This—this works.”

Rachel’s stomach twists hard. “It doesn’t.”

Lena frowns. “We’re out of sight. It’s dry. There are doors.”

Rachel looks at the nearest one. It hangs crooked in its frame, paint blistered, lock either rusted shut or broken clean through. It looks like something that hasn’t kept anyone out in a long time.

Or in.

“We won’t be found here,” Lena says, trying again.

The words scrape.

The ringing hums, closer now, threading into Rachel’s thoughts without effort.

This place doesn’t hold people, it said.

Rachel presses her fingers into her temples. “Don’t.”

That’s why it works, it replied.

She drags her hands down her face, palms smearing rain and dirt, pressure pulsing behind her eyes in time with her heartbeat. “I just want to stop,” she mutters. “Five minutes.”

The ground firms under her foot when she steps forward, the smallest assist, barely there, and she hates that she notices it.

Lena notices anyway. “Rachel.”

“I know,” Rachel says, because she does.

They choose a building that looks like it never wanted company—no lights, no intact glass. The door gives when Lena pushes it, wood creaking in complaint more than resistance. Inside smells like damp and old dust and something metallic that makes Rachel’s tongue taste wrong, and the ringing drops another notch, settling into a low, steady thread that makes the space feel quieter than it should.

Too quiet.

Lena closes the door out of habit, then hesitates before wedging a broken chair against it. “Just in case.”

Rachel doesn’t argue, but her shoulders tighten as the room finishes deciding how it wants to exist around them.

“This isn’t safe,” she says, low.

Lena sits hard on the edge of a collapsed table, exhaustion finally winning. “It’s safe enough.”

The ringing flares, pleased.

No one looks for what doesn’t register, it said.

Rachel closes her eyes and feels it then—the way the place doesn’t line up with her properly, like she’s standing half a step out of phase with the floor, the walls, the air. It doesn’t push back. It doesn’t pull her in.

It just doesn’t care.

And beneath the ache and the fatigue and the noise, the understanding lands heavy and unwelcome:

If she stays somewhere like this too long, nothing will notice when she leaves.

Or when she doesn’t.

She opens her eyes. “We can’t sleep here.”

Lena looks up, startled. “Rachel—”

“Not long,” Rachel says, softening it because Lena needs something workable. “Just… not long.”

The ringing settles again, patient, content.

You’re learning, it said.

Rachel stays on her feet, listening to a place that doesn’t listen back, and knows they’re still moving even now—just more slowly, in the wrong direction.

# Chapter 53 — Noticing

Rachel stays standing.

Lena sinks to the floor with a tired sound, back against the wall, head dropping forward like gravity finally remembered her. The building doesn’t answer that. Air presses in, damp and stale, holding its shape without reacting.

“Just a minute,” Lena says, eyes closed.

Rachel nods because it’s easier than speaking. She keeps her weight angled off her ankle, one foot braced, hands loose at her sides. The ringing sits behind her eyes, thinner than before, stretched tight like it’s being pulled somewhere else.

The room smells layered—wet wood, metal, dust that’s been damp long enough to lose its edges. The walls are close enough to touch without reaching, corners softened where they should meet cleanly.

Rachel shifts her weight.

The floor gives.

Not with sound. Just a brief, unmistakable softness under her foot, like pressure meeting pressure. She freezes, breath catching mid-draw.

The ringing tightens—

—and then slides away.

Not gone. Displaced.

There, it said.

The word lands cleanly.

No spike.  
No pain.  
No distortion.

Rachel’s stomach drops.

She doesn’t move. After a moment the floor firms again, ordinary, pretending nothing happened. She keeps her eyes fixed on the far wall, pulse loud enough to hear.

Lena rubs at her face, still not looking up. “You okay?”

“Yeah,” Rachel says. The word comes out steady, and that scares her more than if it hadn’t. “Just tired.”

She takes a careful step away from the wall.

The space answers faster this time. Air presses faintly around her shoulders, close enough to notice, and she stops instinctively.

The pressure eases.

You don’t need the noise anymore, the voice says, almost conversational.

Rachel swallows. “Stop.”

The voice doesn’t raise itself. It doesn’t push.

I am stopped, it said.

Her chest tightens. The ringing is barely there now, a distant echo, but the voice remains, settled somewhere nearer than it should be.

Rachel looks toward the door. The broken chair leans against it at a useless angle, wood splintered, lock hanging open. It doesn’t feel like an exit. It feels like a suggestion the building hasn’t bothered to argue with.

She shifts her weight again, testing.

The floor responds in the same place, the same fraction, like it remembers her. The ringing doesn’t spike.

The voice doesn’t wait.

You feel that faster now, it said.

Rachel closes her eyes, grounding herself in the smell, the damp, the quiet weight of the room. “It’s just a building.”

A pause.

Not distant.  
Not restrained.

So are you, it replied.

Lena opens her eyes and finally looks at her. “I don’t like this place,” she says. “It’s… empty.”

Rachel almost laughs. “It’s not.”

She presses her foot down deliberately, just enough to feel that wrong give again. The voice stays with her, calm and present.

No excitement.  
No hunger.

Just proximity.

She pulls her foot back.

“We can’t stay,” she says.

Lena exhales, relief cutting through exhaustion. “Good. Because I wasn’t planning on sleeping.”

They move for the door together. Rachel keeps her steps light, careful, aware of how quickly the room seems to register her leaving.

Outside, the night opens back up—rain louder, air thinner, sound less contained.

The ringing is almost gone.

The voice isn’t.

You don’t need it anymore, it says easily.

Rachel steps away from the building without answering.

She already knows that’s the cost.

They don’t go far before Lena stops again, this time without collapsing, hands rubbing together like she’s warming them even though the rain hasn’t changed. Rachel leans her shoulder against the building’s outer wall, careful with her ankle, listening to the way sound carries now that they’re back outside—rain louder, metal ticking somewhere nearby, the open night thinner than it should be.

“Okay,” Lena says. “We need a plan. Not a good one. Just something.”

Rachel nods. Her ankle throbs steadily, manageable, the kind of pain that gives you something solid to hold onto. Lena keeps talking, words stacking one on top of another in that practical way she gets when fear hasn’t finished deciding what to do with itself.

“Road’s bad. Trees aren’t great. We can’t double back.” Lena gestures vaguely, not looking behind them. “We head downhill. Less visibility. Fewer places to get boxed in.”

Rachel listens, following the rhythm more than the meaning, grateful for something ordinary. “Downhill,” she repeats, because repeating feels safer than adding.

The voice slides in.

You won’t make it far like that, it said.

Rachel flinches, not from pain, not from pressure, but because it lands in the same space Lena’s voice just occupied, clean and unmissable. She clamps her mouth shut and stares at the dark ground near her feet, breathing slow through her nose.

Lena keeps going, unaware. “People don’t like walking wet slopes in the dark. It buys us time.”

She’s thinking in straight lines, the voice says, almost fond. That won’t help you.

Rachel nods too fast.

Lena pauses. “You okay? You’re doing that thing again.”

“What thing.”

“Freezing.” Lena studies her face. “You do it when you’re in pain.”

“I’m fine,” Rachel says, because the word is already there, ready. “Just tired.”

The voice doesn’t wait.

You’re tired because you’re carrying it alone, it said.

Rachel’s fingers curl into her palms. She focuses on the scrape of wet leaves under Lena’s boots, the smell of rain and rust and something faintly electrical she doesn’t want to think about.

“We should keep moving,” Lena says. “Before someone notices we didn’t come back.”

They already did, the voice murmurs, calm enough to be cruel.

Rachel’s breath stutters.

Lena’s eyes sharpen. “What.”

“Nothing.” Rachel shakes her head, then forces the movement to slow, to look normal. “Sorry. Say that again.”

“Which part.”

“The—” Rachel stops herself. “Moving.”

Lena nods, relief flickering through her worry. “Okay. Good.”

They start walking. For a few seconds the sound of their steps lines up, rain steady, breathing even. Rachel counts her footfalls, lets the physical work fill as much space as it can.

“If we get separated—” Lena begins.

You won’t, the voice says immediately.

Rachel stops short.

Lena stumbles a step ahead, swears quietly, then turns. “Rachel?”

“I—sorry.” Rachel adjusts her stance, heart racing. “What were you saying.”

“I said we shouldn’t get separated.” Lena watches her carefully now.

Rachel nods. “Right.”

You don’t need to explain that to her, the voice adds. She’ll follow.

The words settle somewhere deep and unwelcome. Rachel presses her tongue to the roof of her mouth, grounding herself in the taste of rain, the ache in her ankle, the fact of her body still moving through space.

Lena steps closer, lowering her voice. “Hey. Talk to me. What’s going on.”

Rachel looks at her—mud on her jeans, rain in her hair, concern open and unguarded—and for a second the voice goes quiet.

Waiting.

“I’m fine,” Rachel says. “I just need to focus.”

Lena hesitates, then nods. “Okay. Just—don’t shut me out.”

Rachel nods again.

They walk.

The voice stays silent for several steps, close enough to feel even without sound.

Then, lightly, like it’s commenting on the weather—

You see how easy that was, it said.

Rachel keeps moving.

The rain keeps falling.

And even without pain, even without the ringing, the space inside her no longer feels empty enough to be her own.

They walk long enough that the rain changes character, thinning and then thickening again as the trees shift around them, branches catching it in uneven patches that leave Rachel soaked one minute and barely damp the next. Her ankle burns steadily now, not sharp enough to stop her, not dull enough to forget, every step a small negotiation she keeps having to win.

Lena keeps pace a half step behind her, quiet, listening. The silence stretches, not empty, just full of things neither of them wants to name yet.

The voice stays with Rachel.

Not speaking.  
Just present.

Lena lifts a hand without turning. “Stop.”

Rachel does, a beat late. Her boot slides before she catches herself. The sound behind them lands again, heavier now, too measured to be rain.

Lena lowers her voice. “That’s footsteps.”

Rachel waits for the ringing.

Nothing comes.

Her throat tightens.

Left, the voice says.

Lena whispers, “Uphill. Trees are thicker.”

Longer, the voice replies, already dismissing it.

Rachel stares into the dark to the left. The ground drops away sharply there, uneven and slick, the kind of descent that hurts if you misjudge it.

“There’s a break,” she says quietly. “We can cut down.”

Lena hesitates. “That’s not safer.”

Rachel turns anyway.

Mud gives under her boots immediately, ankle flaring hot enough to make her gasp. She catches herself on a low branch and keeps moving, heart hammering, breath ragged. Behind her Lena swears and slides, grabbing Rachel’s jacket for a second before letting go and finding her own balance.

They don’t stop until they reach the bottom, crouching behind a tangle of fallen trees and wet brush. Rachel presses her palm into the mud, grounding herself, rain ticking softly against bark overhead.

The footsteps don’t follow.

Lena stays crouched, breathing hard. “You could’ve warned me.”

Rachel nods. “I’m sorry.”

You didn’t stop, the voice says.

Lena looks up at her. “How did you know that was there?”

Rachel opens her mouth. Closes it. Her jaw tightens. “I didn’t. I just… went.”

Lena studies her face longer than she needs to, then looks away. “Next time, say something.”

They move again, slower now, ground flattening into something almost walkable. The rain thins under thicker cover, sound changing shape again. Rachel counts steps, keeps her weight light, lets the ache in her ankle pull her attention outward instead of in.

The voice breaks the quiet.

Not the path, it said.

Ahead, a narrow trail cuts through the trees, worn enough to be visible even in the dark.

“That’s the first clear thing we’ve seen,” Lena says. “That’s actual ground.”

Rachel hesitates.

They’ll expect it, the voice says, calm.

She angles left.

Lena stops short. “Rachel.”

Rachel keeps going, brushing past low branches, ground narrowing fast enough that Lena has to follow single-file.

“Hey.” Lena catches her arm. “Stop. You’re limping worse. You’re not thinking straight.”

The contact is warm, real. For a second the voice goes quiet.

Rachel breathes.

Rain.  
Mud.
